The successor does not inherit the Founder-specific privileges under Article VI.3, including but not limited to direct control of the Founder Family Trust, the unique title "Founder & Steward President," the no-salary provision, the power to make first generation appointments (as those appointments are already made by the Founder), and the Founder's absolute veto (the successor's veto may be overridden as provided in Article VI.2(e)).

The position of Life Visionary (and any subsequent Steward President) shall receive salary and benefits as defined by the Foundation's remuneration policy.

7.4 Term, Renewal & Succession

(a) The Life Visionary (who, upon succession, becomes the Second or subsequent Steward President) shall serve a term of ten (10) years, renewable once for a maximum of twenty (20) consecutive years.

(b) Renewal requires positive evaluation by the Family Council and acknowledgement by both Trusts.

(c) Upon term expiration, a new Life Visionary shall be nominated and elected as detailed in confidential ANNEX A, and shall become the next numbered Steward President.

(d) Upon vacancy, the Founder's family council shall nominate a successor within ninety (90) days, and the Trusts shall acknowledge the nomination based on alignment with the Founder's vision. Any dispute between Trusts regarding nomination or succession shall be resolved through binding mediation as detailed in confidential ANNEX F.

7.5 Explicit Limitations

The Life Visionary (and any subsequent Steward President) shall not:

(a) Amend or initiate amendments to any Core Articles except through the procedure in Article XIX;

(b) Interfere with or overrule Board or management operational decisions except through the inherited guardian powers under Article VI.2;

(c) Access or direct Foundation funds, assets, or contracts for personal benefit;

(d) Hold, control, or be the registered owner of any Founder Shares or any equity interest in the Holding Company or Operating Companies;

(e) Exercise control over the Founder Family Trust, which remains separate and under the control of the Founder's family as provided in Article XXX and the Family Protocol.

7.6 Removal for Cause

The Life Visionary (or any subsequent Steward President holding the office after the Founder) may be removed before term expiration only for grave cause (as defined in ANNEX A) by the process set in the public referendum for removal of the holder of the Steward office under Article XLIV. No body, board, or Trust  may personally remove the holder of the Steward Office.

7.7 Life Visionary as ex-officio Trustee & Dispute Resolution

The Life Visionary (and any subsequent Steward President) shall serve as a non-executive, ex-officio Trustee of the Jowih Purpose Trust (Article XXIX) for the duration of their term. Any disputes arising from nomination, election, or removal shall be resolved per the process outlined in confidential ANNEX A (internal mediation to binding arbitration).

7.8 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3.

ARTICLE VIII - SUPREME GUARDIANSHIP & EXECUTIVE LEADERSHIP

Structural Authority and Separation of Powers The Founder shall exercise strategic influence over the Foundation solely through the office of the Steward President, who shall serve as the singular conduit of Founder lineage within the Foundation itself, with no other member of the Founder’s lineage permitted to hold any positions therein; the Foundation shall remain institutionally independent in its operations, free from direct familial concentration or interference; ownership and oversight of the Holding Company shall reside with the Purpose Trust and the Founder Trust, acting through their professional appointed representatives and without engaging in direct operational management; no person serving within the Foundation, the Purpose Trust, the Founder Trust, or the Holding Company Board shall participate in, influence, or exercise authority over the appointment, supervision, evaluation, or advancement of any related individual, nor shall any combination of related persons be permitted to concentrate influence across these three governance structures; all familial relationships must be fully disclosed and shall be subject to strict review by the Independent Governance & Ethics Committee and any attempt to practice nepotism shall constitute a breach of governance integrity subject to immediate removal and this is absolute; this structure shall ensure that influence is centralized, oversight is indirect but effective, and execution remains professional and independent at all times.

8.1 The Steward President

The Steward President is the permanent guardian of the Foundation's mission and constitution, with supreme authority over the Foundation's objectives and strategic alignment, as provided in Article VIII.1 of the original Constitution. The First Steward President is the Founder. Subsequent Steward Presidents are numbered (Second, Third, etc.) and bear the honorific "Life Visionary."

The First Steward President (Founder) shall not receive any salary for holding this office, as provided in Article VIII.1(e) of the original Constitution. Subsequent Steward Presidents (Second, Third, etc.) may receive salary and benefits as determined by the Remuneration Committee, subject to Article III (Non-Profit & No-Equity Policy).

8.2 The Life Visionary 

The Life Visionary is the designated successor to the Steward President, appointed by the Founder & Steward President or through an election process in accordance with the Family Protocol and ANNEX A, as provided in Article VIII.2 of the original Constitution.

The Life Visionary has no authority to act on behalf of the Steward President during the Founder's lifetime except where expressly delegated in writing by the Founder & Steward President. There is no "Vice Steward" or deputy role.

 The successor Steward President is bound by the same constitutional limitations as the Founder, except as expressly provided otherwise in this Constitution.

8.3 Foundation CEO

(a) The Foundation CEO is the chief executive responsible for day-to-day operations, implementing the strategy set by the Steward President, and managing the executive team, as provided in Article VIII.3 of the original Constitution.

(b) The Foundation CEO is appointed by the Steward President for a term of seven (7) years, renewable once, and may be removed by the Steward President at any time for cause, as provided in Article VIII.3(b).

(c) The Foundation CEO reports directly to the Steward President.

(d) The Foundation CEO shall form an Executive Team with clearly defined roles and lean team structures as outlined in Article X.

8.4 Steward President Public Presence

(a) Principle of Strategic Presence:  The Steward President maintains a deliberate and selective public presence. His/Her appearances are reserved for matters of continental,strategic, and institutional significance.

(b) Nature of Appearances

    The Steward President presides over:

    (i) Launch and inauguration of major continental projects;

    (ii) Reception of visiting heads of state or government at the Jowih Citadel;

    (iii) Signing of ADFA agreements with new signatory states;

    (iv) Annual convocations and high-level institutional gatherings;

    (v) Major milestone announcements;

    (vi) Moments requiring direct stewardship in protection of the Mission;

    (vii) Other engagements of comparable significance.

(c) Operational Communications

Routine communications, operational updates, and day-to-day media engagement are handled by the Foundation’s Spokesperson.

(d) Political Neutrality in Appearance

The Steward President shall not appear at political rallies, campaignevents, or partisan gatherings, in accordance with Article XLVII.

(e) Institutional, Not Personal, Visibility

The Steward President’s public presence serves the Foundation. It is not used for personal promotion, commercial endorsement, or the cultivation of an individual public persona.

Article IX - Oversight & Independent Audit

9.1 Oversight Committee (3 Members)

a) The Oversight Committee is composed of 3 members appointed by the Founder  and future members are selected by the Combined Two Trusts for 7 years renewable once.

b) The Oversight Committee is responsible for:

   i. Reviewing compliance, budgets, and alignment with the Foundation's objectives.

   ii. Escalating breaches or irregularities to the Steward President.

   iii. Recommending corrective actions to the Steward President and Foundation CEO.

   iv. Overseeing the Foundation's risk management framework.

   v. Recommending removal of the Foundation CEO to the Steward President and the Two Trusts.

c) The Oversight Committee has no executive authority and cannot remove the Steward President and IAG.

d) At least one member must be independent (not a Foundation employee or contractor).

9.2 Independent Auditor General (IAG)

The Independent Auditor General (IAG) Office, powers, safeguards, audit supremacy mandate, funding guarantees, identity protection provisions, sub-office structure, security controls, movement restrictions, succession planning, and compromise-prevention mechanisms shall be governed in full by Article XLIII (Audit Supremacy & The Independent Auditor General) and the classified procedures contained in ANNEX B.

i. Where any interpretive conflict exists between this Article 9.2 and Article XLIII, Article XLIII shall prevail.

ii. Selection, vetting, security screening, identity redaction periods, foreign influence restrictions, monitoring protocols, and successor preparation timelines are exclusively defined in ANNEX B and are not required to be restated in public constitutional text.

iii. Operational audit authority described in this Article is deemed automatically expanded to the full constitutional scope granted under Article XLIII.

iv. All IAG Sub-Offices, field integrity units, and specialized audit functions operate under Article XLIII and ANNEX B authority while remaining functionally linked to this Article 9.2 mandate.

9.4 Collaboration

The Oversight Committee and the Independent Auditor General shall collaborate as needed, but maintain independence in their functions. Both bodies report separately to the Steward President.

9.5 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein.

ARTICLE X - EXECUTIVE MANAGEMENT

10.1 CEO Authority & Limits

a) The Foundation CEO exercises authority over the day-to-day operations of the Foundation, excluding New Dawn City and the commercial operations of Jowih Holdings Limited and the Jowih Group, implementing strategy as set by the Steward President and in accordance with this Constitution.

b) The CEO's authority operates within defined governance frameworks, including:

i. the Delegation of Authority (DoA) established by the Steward President;ii. spending parameters set within the approved annual budget;iii. constitutional provisions safeguarding neutrality and preventing mission drift; andiv. team structure and size limitations.

c) In exercising this authority, the CEO shall ensure that all Foundation operations remain aligned with the strategic direction and intent of the Mission.

d) New Dawn City operates as a distinct governance domain. The Foundation CEO holds no operational authority within New Dawn City, which is administered by the New Dawn City Administrator in accordance with Article LVII.

e) Nothing in this Article shall be interpreted as granting the Foundation CEO operational command over Jowih Holdings Limited, the Jowih Group, or any Operating Company, except to the extent that this Constitution expressly provides for Foundation oversight, mission alignment, funding, or other reserved authority.

10.2 Foundation Mission Coordination Functions

a) The Foundation shall maintain a lean mission-coordination structure responsible for strategic coordination, standards, policy development, mission oversight, continental alignment, and support for implementation across the Foundation's mission areas.

b) The Foundation shall not duplicate the operational management functions of Jowih Holdings Limited, Jowih Group, or the Operating Companies.

c) The Foundation's mission-coordination functions shall include:

i. Infrastructure & Economic Development Coordination - establishes mission priorities, standards, and policy frameworks for infrastructure and economic development, while operational execution is undertaken through Jowih Holdings Limited and the Jowih Group;

ii. Health & Social Development Coordination - establishes mission priorities, standards, beneficiary protections, and policy frameworks for health and social development, while operational execution of commercial and operating entities is undertaken through the Jowih Group;

iii. Education & Workforce Coordination - establishes mission priorities, standards, workforce-development policy, and program frameworks, while operational execution of education enterprises and related entities is undertaken through the Jowih Group;

iv. Housing & Urban Development Coordination - establishes mission priorities, affordability principles, beneficiary protections, and urban-development standards, while operational execution is undertaken through the Jowih Group and applicable city structures;

v. Agriculture, Environment & Sustainability Coordination - establishes mission standards, sustainability requirements, and policy frameworks, while commercial and infrastructure execution is undertaken through the Jowih Group;

vi. Technology, Research & Innovation Coordination - establishes mission-aligned technology policy, strategic priorities, ethical standards, and coordination with the Jowih Group's research and innovation system, while research, technology development, commercialization, and spin-off execution shall be undertaken through the Jowih Group;

vii. Finance & Mission Allocation Coordination - oversees Foundation funding policy, Mission Fund allocation, Country Budget Pots, grants, reserves, and other Foundation financial functions, while commercial finance, investment, treasury, and operating capital management shall be undertaken through the Jowih Holdings Limited and Jowih Group structures; and

viii. Legal, Governance & Continental Coordination - coordinates constitutional compliance, ADFA, HGA frameworks, Foundation legal matters, dispute-resolution frameworks, and governance standards.

d) The functions described in this Article are coordination and mission-governance functions and shall not constitute operational command over Jowih Holdings Limited, the Jowih Group, or any Operating Company.

e) The Foundation CEO may establish appropriate internal offices, units, or designated officers to perform these functions, subject to the staffing and headcount controls established under Article XI.

10.3 Regional Executives

a) The Foundation shall appoint five Regional Executives for:

i. North Africa;ii. West Africa;iii. East Africa;iv. Central Africa; andv. Southern Africa.

b) Regional Executives shall coordinate Foundation mission implementation across countries within their respective regions and shall report to the Foundation CEO.

c) Regional Executives shall support PAN-Ambassadors and ensure alignment with continental Foundation standards.

d) Regional Executives shall not exercise operational command over Jowih Holdings Limited, Jowih Group, Operating Companies, or their executives.

e) Regional Executives may facilitate coordination between Foundation structures, participating States, and Jowih Group entities where authorized, but such facilitation shall not alter the governance or reporting structures of the Jowih Group.

10.4 Foundation CEO Executive Office

a) The Foundation CEO shall maintain a lean executive office focused exclusively on Foundation mission delivery, coordination, administration, financial allocation, integrity, programs, and country support.

b) The Foundation CEO's immediate office shall comprise no more than:

i. Foundation CEO;ii. Executive Assistant; andiii. Strategy Analyst,

subject to the staffing provisions of Article XI.

c) The principal CEO-support departments shall comprise:

i. Chief Finance & Allocation Office;ii. Chief Integrity & Compliance Office;iii. Secretariat;iv. Programs Department; andv. Country Support Unit.

d) No CEO Executive Office department shall be construed as a substitute for or duplicate of the Group Executive Structure established under Article 28.5.

e) Any increase beyond the applicable Foundation staffing limits shall require the approvals prescribed by this Constitution.

10.5 Public Service Fellows

(a) PAN-Ambassadors and Country Executive Directors are designated as full-time, professional roles compensated under the Public Service Fellows Compensation Framework as adopted by the Combined Trusts Board.

(b) FTE Status: Public Service Fellows are counted as employees for compensation purposes but are excluded from the Foundation's administrative FTE cap under Section 10.4(e) because their compensation is funded through Country Budget Pots (Article LIII) or the Mission Fund, not the central administrative budget.

(c) Compensation: Public Service Fellows shall receive:(i) A professional base stipend determined by country tier (A, B, C) as defined in the Public Service Fellows Compensation Framework;(ii) Performance bonuses tied to independently verified milestone achievement;(iii) Health insurance, evacuation insurance, and workers' compensation;(iv) Defined-contribution pension vesting after 7 years of service.

(d) No Equity: Nothing in this Section creates or implies any equity, shareholding, ownership, or profit-sharing interest in the Foundation, any Trust, or any Operating Company.

(e) Term Limits: PAN-Ambassadors serve a maximum of two consecutive 7-year terms. Country Executive Directors serve a maximum of three consecutive 4-year terms. Cooling-off periods apply as defined in the Public Service Fellows Compensation Framework.

(f) Prohibition on Outside Employment: Public Service Fellows are full-time roles. No Fellow may engage in outside employment, consulting, or business activities that interfere with Foundation duties or present a conflict of interest.

10.6 Human Resources Policy

(a) The Foundation shall adopt a Human Resources Policy compliant with international best practices.

(b) All staff shall be hired on merit, with mandatory background checks for roles involving beneficiaries.

(c) Staff remuneration shall be reasonable, based on non-profit market rates, and approved by the Steward President.

(d) No staff member shall receive dividends or profits from Foundation activities.

(e) Annual Staffing Plan: Each department head must submit an annual staffing plan to the CEO, who consolidates and presents it to the Steward President for approval. The plan must include:(i) Justification for each position;(ii) Impact metrics for each role;(iii) Analysis of administrative efficiency;(iv) Succession planning for key roles.

10.7 Termination and Discipline

(a) Staff may be terminated for cause (misconduct, breach of duties) by the CEO with Steward President approval. Public Service Fellows may be removed only under the procedures in Article LIV and the Public Service Fellows Compensation Framework.

(b) Appeals may be made to the Oversight Committee and ultimately to the Steward President.

(c) Gross misconduct includes:(i) Creating or maintaining ghost workers;(ii) Engaging in project cycling for funding;(iii) Violating constitutional prohibitions on political activity;(iv) Exceeding departmental FTE limits without approval;(v) Falsifying staffing justifications.

10.8 Technology Optimization Requirement

(a) Each department shall continuously assess how technology can reduce administrative burden while increasing efficiency.

(b) The Foundation shall invest in automation and AI systems to handle routine administrative tasks through the Governance OS.

(c) Self-service platforms shall be developed to empower staff and reduce coordination workload.

(d) Annual technology investment shall be at least 10% of administrative budget to ensure continuous efficiency improvements.

10.9 Performance Metrics & Efficiency Targets

(a) Each department shall maintain administrative efficiency ratio below 15% (admin costs vs. program delivery).

(b) Span of control: Each manager shall supervise at least 3-5 direct reports to ensure lean structure.

(c) Process automation: Departments shall target 30% reduction in manual processes annually.

(d) Volunteer leverage: Each paid FTE (excluding Public Service Fellows) shall support at least 10 active Comrade Hub volunteers.

10.10 Succession & Knowledge Management

(a) Each executive role must have at least one trained backup.

(b) Knowledge transfer protocols must be documented for all critical functions.

(c) Cross-training between departments shall be encouraged to maximize flexibility.

(d) Institutional memory shall be systematically captured in the Foundation's knowledge base.

10.11 Operating Company Innovation Framework

The Foundation shall maintain confidential ANNEX O (Operating Company Innovation Framework) containing detailed provisions governing employee innovation, breakthrough determinations, royalty structures, and reward mechanisms for employees of all Operating Companies. ANNEX O is incorporated by reference and has binding legal effect. Notwithstanding ANNEX O, no employee shall receive equity, shares, or ownership in any entity. All intellectual property belongs irrevocably to the Foundation or Jowih IP Holdings. This Article is a Tier 2 Core Article.

10.12 R&D Hub Research Framework

The Foundation shall maintain confidential ANNEX R (Research & Development Framework) containing detailed provisions governing the six Continental R&D Hubs, including researcher compensation, royalty formulas, spin-off entity formation, patent allocation, and commercialization pathways. ANNEX R is incorporated by reference and has binding legal effect. All intellectual property belongs irrevocably to Jowih IP Holdings. This Article is a Tier 2 Core Article.

10.13New Dawn City Governance Integration

(a) The New Dawn City Administrator is not part of the Foundation CEO's executive team. The Administrator reports directly to the Steward President as provided in Article LVII.

(b) New Dawn City executives are not counted under the Foundation's administrative FTE cap. They are funded through New Dawn City revenue and governed by the New Dawn City Administrator under Article LVII.

(c) The Foundation CEO and the New Dawn City Administrator shall coordinate on:(i) Workforce deployment (Comrade Hub graduates entering New Dawn City);(ii) Technology transfer from R&D Centers into New Dawn City;(iii) Replication of New Dawn City models to other cities;(iv) Budget alignment between Mission Fund and New Dawn City revenue.

(d) Disputes in coordination shall be resolved by the Steward President, whose decision is final and binding.

10.14 Communications and Spokesperson

(a) The Foundation shall maintain a Communications Office responsible for all public communications, media relations, and information dissemination.

(b) A designated Spokesperson shall serve as the primary public interface for routine media engagement, press briefings, and official statements on behalf of the Foundation.

(c) The Spokesperson communicates positions that are:(i) Approved by the Foundation CEO for operational matters; and(ii) Aligned with the strategic direction of the Steward President.

(d) The Spokesperson does not set policy, interpret strategy independently, or act beyond authorized communications.

(e) The Steward President and the Foundation CEO may communicate directly where appropriate, in accordance with their respective roles.

(f) All major announcements - including new partnerships, ADFA agreements, bond issuances, and continental initiatives - shall be made by the Steward President or through the Spokesperson with the Steward President's explicit authorization.

(g) The Communications Office shall coordinate with the IAG to ensure all public communications comply with transparency requirements and do not compromise security or ongoing investigations.

Article XI - Responsible Staffing & Anti-Ghost Worker Safeguards

XI.1 Principle of Lean and Efficient Staffing   All hiring decisions must be justified by a clear and present operational need directly tied to the Foundation's strategic objectives as defined in Article II. The Foundation and all Operating Companies shall prioritize leveraging technology and the Comrade Hub network (per Article XXIII) to scale impact without unnecessary administrative bloat.  

XI.2 Mandatory Staffing Plan and Headcount Cap  

a) Annual Staffing Plan: The CEO (for Foundation) or equivalent executive (for each Operating Company) shall prepare an Annual Staffing Plan for approval by their respective Board or the Jowih Purpose Trust. This plan must detail:  

 i. Proposed new hires, with written justifications linking each role to specific strategic outcomes.  

 ii. The total full-time equivalent (FTE) employee count and total personnel budget.  

 iii. An organizational chart demonstrating clear reporting lines and spans of control.  

b) The Foundation shall maintain lean operations with headcount caps set annually based on operational requirements, guided by the principle that core administrative functions shall not exceed 10 FTE without supermajority Board approval. Operating Companies may establish separate, justified caps appropriate to their commercial scale.

XI.3 Anti-Ghost Worker & Payroll Integrity Measures  

a) Definition: A "Ghost Worker" is defined as any individual who receives compensation from the Foundation or any Operating Company without performing bona fide services, including but not limited to fictitious employees, departed employees kept on payroll, or individuals receiving salaries for no-show jobs.  

b) Pre-Hire Verification: No individual may be added to the payroll without:  

 i. A signed employment contract.  

 ii. Completed personnel file with verified credentials.  

 iii. Formal onboarding documented by the HR department.  

c) Biometric & Continuous Verification: For all operational offices, the Foundation and Operating Companies shall implement a secure, privacy-compliant biometric (or similar robust) attendance system through the Governance OS. All staff must be physically verified as active at least once per payroll cycle.  

d) Segregation of Duties: The functions of hiring, payroll processing, and attendance verification shall be separated among different departments to prevent collusion.  

XI.4 Independent Auditor General's Mandate for Payroll Audit  

a) The Independent Auditor General (IAG), under their mandate in Article XLIII, shall conduct quarterly random payroll audits across the Foundation and all Operating Companies.  

b) These audits shall specifically verify the existence and active employment of a statistically significant sample of staff, cross-referencing payroll records with attendance data, work output, and bank payment confirmations.  

c) The IAG has the authority to initiate unannounced physical headcounts and desk audits to verify employee presence.  

XI.5 Consequences for Violations  

a) Gross Misconduct: Knowingly creating or maintaining a ghost worker, or approving hires in violation of the staffing cap without authorization, constitutes gross misconduct and immediate grounds for dismissal, as per Article 10.7.  

b) Financial Liability: Any individual found to have facilitated ghost workers shall be personally liable for the full restitution of all fraudulently paid funds, plus associated costs.  

c) Criminal Referral: All instances of suspected ghost worker fraud shall be reported to law enforcement authorities.  

d) Managerial Accountability: Department heads who exceed their approved staffing budget without authorization shall be subject to disciplinary action, including suspension of hiring privileges and financial penalties.  

XI.6 Public Transparency  

The Foundation's Annual Impact Report (Article XXXV) shall include a summary of its total FTE count, personnel costs as a percentage of total expenses, and a note confirming the IAG's review of payroll integrity. Each Operating Company shall provide similar summaries to the Foundation for integration into the report, ensuring transparency across all entities.  

XI.7 Prohibition on Project Cycling for Funding  

a) Prohibition: The Foundation and Operating Companies shall not engage in or support Project Cycling. All projects must be initiated with a clear timeline, measurable milestones, and completion criteria documented in the Annual Staffing Plan or budget (per Article V). Relaunches or extensions require Board approval with evidence of prior value delivered and a root-cause analysis of any stalls.  

b) Staffing Integration: No new hires or resource allocations shall be approved for relaunched projects suspected of cycling. The IAG shall audit project histories during payroll reviews to detect patterns of cycling.  

c) Consequences: Engaging in Project Cycling constitutes gross misconduct, subject to the sanctions in XI.5, including restitution of misallocated funds and potential criminal referral if fraudulent intent is found.  

XI.8 Application Across Entities  These measures apply across all entities, with the IAG (Article XLIII) auditing compliance to ensure independence does not lead to bloat or mission drift.

XI.9 Administrative Efficiency Ratio: The Foundation shall target maintaining administrative costs below 15% of total program expenditures, with any variance above 20% triggering mandatory operational review.

XI.10  Clarification on Volunteer Leadership & the Africa Development Network (ADU)

a) The headcount caps and FTE definitions in this Article apply to salaried employees of the Jowih Foundation and its Operating Companies.

b) Volunteer Leaders may receive a modest stipend, determined by the Remuneration Committee, to cover communication, transportation, and time costs, along with reimbursement for pre-approved, mission-essential expenses. These stipends shall be categorized as "Program Implementation" costs, not "Staff Salaries."

c) The work of Volunteer Leaders shall be amplified and supported by the volunteer network of the Comrade Hub (Article XXIII), ensuring the ADN operates on a principle of distributed, community-sourced effort, not a traditional bloated administrative structure.

e) The Foundation's core administrative staff is responsible for providing centralized support, systems, and direct disbursement execution (per Article LIII) to the ADN's Volunteer Leaders.

XI. Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 2 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein.

Article XII - Indemnification and Limitation of Liability

12.1 Indemnification: The Foundation shall indemnify and hold harmless, to the fullest extent permitted by law, any Governor, Officer, employee, or agent against all expenses, judgments, fines, settlements, and other amounts actually and reasonably incurred in connection with any proceeding arising from their good-faith performance of duties for the Foundation, provided they acted honestly and in the best interests of the Foundation.  

12.2 Limitation of Liability: No Governor or Officer shall be personally liable for any debt, liability, or obligation of the Foundation solely by reason of being a Governor or Officer, except in cases of willful misconduct, gross negligence, or breach of fiduciary duty as determined by a court.  

12.3 Insurance: The Foundation shall maintain directors' and officers' liability insurance as part of its insurance obligations under Article XVII.  

12.4 Advancement of Expenses: The Foundation may advance expenses for defense upon receipt of an undertaking by or on behalf of the indemnified person to repay such amount if it is ultimately determined that the person is not entitled to be indemnified by the Foundation.  

12.5 Non-Exclusivity: The rights to indemnification and advancement of expenses conferred in this Article shall not be exclusive of any other right which any person may have or hereafter acquire under any statute, the Foundation's Constitution, agreement, vote of Members or disinterested Governors, or otherwise.  

12.6 Survival: The indemnification and advancement of expenses provided by this Article shall continue as to a person who has ceased to be a Governor, Officer, employee, or agent and shall inure to the benefit of the heirs, executors, and administrators of such a person.  

12.7 Amendments: Any repeal or modification of this Article shall not adversely affect any right or protection of a Governor, Officer, employee, or agent existing at the time of such repeal or modification.  

12.8 Severability: If any provision of this Article is held to be invalid or unenforceable, the remaining provisions shall remain in full force and effect, and the invalid provision shall be deemed modified to the minimum extent necessary to make it valid and enforceable.  

ARTICLE XIII - DIRECT PROCUREMENT, EXECUTION & STRATEGIC PARTNERSHIPS

13.1 Principle of Direct Execution

13.1.1 The Foundation is a direct executor of its mission, not a pass-through funder or contract manager. The default model is direct execution by Foundation personnel, Exchange programme graduates, Comrade Hub workforce, and Foundation-controlled entities.

13.1.2 Third-party contractors shall be engaged only where:

(a) Specialized expertise or equipment is not available within the Foundation;

(b) Geographic scale requires local presence that the Foundation has not yet established;

(c) Emergency circumstances under Article XLVI require immediate deployment;

(d) Strategic partnerships under Article 13.5 provide technology transfer or capacity building benefits that outweigh direct execution.

13.1.3 The Foundation shall maintain a bias toward building internal capacity rather than outsourcing.

13.2 Direct Procurement

13.2.1 When the Foundation procures goods, equipment, or materials for direct execution, procurement shall be:

(a) Transparent and documented;

(b) Competitive where multiple suppliers exist;

(c) Logged in the Governance OS with immutable timestamps.

13.2.2 The Foundation shall prioritize:

(a) African suppliers where quality and cost are competitive;

(b) Suppliers that demonstrate commitment to local content and workforce development;

(c) Suppliers that sign Contributor Trust Agreements (Article XVI.2) where intellectual property is involved.

13.2.3 All procurement actions shall be subject to IAG audit.

13.3 Limited Engagement of Contractors

13.3.1 When the Foundation engages third-party contractors, the following shall apply:

(a) Contracts shall be time-bound and scope-limited;

(b) Contractors shall comply with the Foundation's Vendor Code of Conduct (Article 13.7);

(c) Contractors shall hire certified Comrade Hub members for a minimum percentage of positions, as determined by the Foundation;

(d) Contractors shall provide on-the-job training to Comrade Hub members;

(e) Contractors shall not subcontract without Foundation approval.

13.3.2 The Foundation shall not enter into contracts that:

(a) Transfer operational control to the contractor;

(b) Grant the contractor intellectual property rights (Article XVI);

(c) Lock the Foundation into long-term exclusivity;

(d) Prevent the Foundation from building its own capacity in the relevant area.

13.3.3 All contractor engagements shall be published on the Public Dashboard, including scope, duration, and value.

13.4 Strategic Partnerships

13.4.1 Strategic partnerships with technology companies, research institutions, and foreign entities shall be governed by Article XX.6 (Technology Transfer as Default Condition).

13.4.2 Such partnerships shall include:

(a) Binding technology transfer agreements;

(b) Joint intellectual property provisions consistent with Article XVI;

(c) Training and capacity building commitments;

(d) Local content and local manufacturing requirements where feasible.

13.4.3 The Foundation shall not enter into exclusive strategic partnerships that would prevent it from partnering with other entities in the same sector (consistent with Article XX.4).

13.4.4 All strategic partnership agreements shall require Steward Office approval, IAG approval, and a written agreement that includes anti-corruption clauses and compliance with this Constitution.

13.5 ADFA Execution (Article XX.9)

13.5.1 Under the Accelerated Development Framework Agreement (ADFA), the Foundation executes projects directly as provided in Article XX.9.8.

13.5.2 Direct execution by the Foundation under the ADFA does not require competitive tender or contractor engagement.

13.5.3 Where contractors are engaged for specialized components of ADFA projects, the requirements of Article 13.3 shall apply.

13.6 Conflict of Interest

13.6.1 All governors, officers, staff, and partners shall declare any actual, potential, or perceived conflict of interest.

13.6.2 Any transaction with a related party (including board members, officers, staff, or their family members) shall require:

(a) Board approval (with the conflicted party recused);

(b) Independent valuation to confirm fair market value;

(c) Public disclosure of the transaction and the conflict.

13.6.3 Failure to disclose a conflict of interest shall constitute gross misconduct under Article XXII.

13.7 Vendor and Contractor Code of Conduct

13.7.1 All vendors and contractors engaged by the Foundation shall sign the Foundation's Vendor Code of Conduct, which includes:

(a) Anti-corruption and anti-bribery provisions;

(b) Child labor prohibition;

(c) Environmental compliance;

(d) Data protection and confidentiality;

(e) Compliance with the Foundation's political neutrality;

(f) Commitment to Comrade Hub hiring and training.

13.7.2 Violation of the Vendor Code of Conduct shall constitute grounds for immediate termination, blacklisting, and legal action.

13.7.3 The Foundation shall maintain a blacklist of vendors and contractors who have violated the Code of Conduct.

13.8 Transparency and Public Access

13.8.1 The Foundation shall maintain a public Procurement and Partnerships Portal as part of the Public Dashboard (Article XXXV), showing:

(a) Direct procurement actions above de minimis threshold;

(b) Contractor engagements, including scope, duration, and value;

(c) Strategic partnership agreements (redacted for confidentiality where necessary);

(d) Contractor performance and compliance.

13.8.2 The IAG shall audit the Portal annually for completeness and accuracy.

13.9 Reporting and Annual Review

13.9.1 The Foundation shall publish an annual Procurement and Partnerships Report, including:

(a) Total value of direct procurement, by category and region;

(b) Total value of contractor engagements, with justification for each;

(c) Local content achieved;

(d) Workforce development outcomes from contractor hiring;

(e) Conflicts disclosed and managed.

13.9.2 The Annual Report shall be included in the Annual Impact Report (Article XXXV).

13.10 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 2 Core Article under Article XIX.3, requiring supermajority approval for amendments.

Article XIV - Safeguarding, Child Protection & Beneficiary Selection

14.1 Commitment to Child Safeguarding The Foundation shall adopt and enforce a Child Safeguarding Policy consistent with international standards (UNICEF/WHO guidance), including background checks, training, child-friendly reporting, and survivor support.  

14.2 Beneficiary Selection Principles Selection of beneficiaries shall be guided by objective needs assessments, case management, and non-discrimination. Reunification with families is prioritized where appropriate.  

14.3 Complaints, Investigations & Whistleblowing A protected whistleblower mechanism with anonymous reporting, investigation protocols, and no-retaliation guarantees shall be established. Findings and remediations will be published redacting sensitive personal data.  

Article XV - Data Protection & Privacy

15.1 Data Governance The Foundation shall maintain a Data Protection Policy compliant under Article XXXIX - Technology & Data Integrity (Modern Compliance) to secure personal data of beneficiaries, staff and users.  

15.2 User Transparency Data collected for donors, app-users and beneficiaries shall be used only for stated purposes and reported in privacy notices. The Dashboard shall preserve privacy: financial transparency without revealing personal identified donor details unless expressly consented.  

ARTICLE XVI - INTELLECTUAL PROPERTY & SOVEREIGN INNOVATION

16.1 IP Ownership

16.1.1 All intellectual property created by or for the Foundation or partners, including but not limited to branding, software, algorithms, research data, patents, trademarks, trade secrets, designs, and proprietary processes, shall be owned by the Foundation or licensed to it under terms protecting the Mission.

16.1.2 The Jowih IP Company (Singapore), a 100% owned subsidiary of Jowih Holdings Limited, shall serve as the central repository and holding entity for all Foundation intellectual property.

16.1.3 The following categories of intellectual property shall be owned exclusively by the Jowih IP Company:

(a) All trademarks, service marks, and branding associated with "Jowih" and the Foundation;

(b) All patents and patent applications arising from Foundation research, development, or strategic acquisitions;

(c) All software code, source code, and digital platforms developed for or by the Foundation, including the Jowih COS and Governance OS;

(d) All research data, algorithms, and proprietary methodologies developed by Foundation R&D hubs or the sensitive research hubs

(e) All trade secrets, know-how, and confidential processes developed in the course of Foundation activities;

(f) All copyrights and creative works produced for or by the Foundation.

16.1.4 No employee, contractor, researcher, volunteer, or affiliate of the Foundation may claim personal ownership of any intellectual property created using Foundation resources, facilities, or time.

16.2 Contributor Trust Agreement

16.2.1 All persons who contribute to the creation of Foundation intellectual property - including employees, contractors, researchers, volunteers, and partners - shall sign a Contributor Trust Agreement.

16.2.2 The Contributor Trust Agreement shall:

(a) Assign all rights, title, and interest in the contributed intellectual property to the Jowih IP Company;

(b) Waive any moral rights to the extent permissible by law;

(c) Acknowledge that no equity, shares, or ownership in any Foundation entity accrues from such contribution;

(d) Submit to binding arbitration for any disputes regarding IP ownership.

16.2.3 Failure to sign a Contributor Trust Agreement shall disqualify any person from participating in Foundation research, development, or creative activities.

16.3 Licensing Framework

16.3.1 The Foundation may license its intellectual property to third parties only under terms that preserve the Asset Lock (Article XXV) and Mission priorities (Article II).

16.3.2 Licenses shall be categorized as follows:

(a) Open Licenses: For non-core, educational, or public benefit IP, the Foundation may use open-source or Creative Commons licenses to maximize continental impact.

(b) Commercial Licenses: For revenue-generating IP, the Foundation may license to third parties at fair market value, with all proceeds flowing to the Mission Fund.

(c) Strategic Licenses: For IP critical to continental sovereignty, the Foundation may license exclusively to African entities or state-owned enterprises at preferential rates.

(d) Internal Licenses: For operating companies and subsidiaries, licenses shall be granted automatically but subject to mission alignment review.

16.3.3 No license shall permit sub-licensing to foreign entities without Foundation approval.

16.3.4 No license shall permit the transfer, sale, or encumbrance of Foundation IP as collateral or security.

16.4 Technology Transfer and African Capacity Building

16.4.1 As provided in Article 20.6, the Foundation shall require technology transfer agreements as a default condition of foreign partnerships.

16.4.2 Technology transfer agreements shall:

(a) Provide African engineers, technicians, and researchers with access to relevant intellectual property under fair and non-discriminatory terms;

(b) Establish local manufacturing, assembly, or maintenance capacity;

(c) Include training and skills development components;

(d) Permit the Foundation to sub-license technology to African entities for public benefit purposes.

16.4.3 The Foundation shall maintain a Technology Transfer Registry, documenting all technology transfer agreements and their outcomes.

16.5 Patent Strategy

16.5.1 The Foundation shall pursue an aggressive patent strategy to protect African innovation and prevent exploitation by anyone.

16.5.2 Patents shall be filed in:

(a) Key African jurisdictions through the African Regional Intellectual Property Organization (ARIPO) and Organisation Africaine de la Propriété Intellectuelle (OAPI);

(b) Major international markets (United States, European Union, China, India, Brazil) to prevent hostile patenting of African innovations;

(c) Such other jurisdictions as the Foundation deems strategic.

16.5.3 The Foundation shall not enforce patents against:

(a) African non-profit organizations or public institutions using the technology for public benefit;

(b) Academic or research institutions for non-commercial research;

(c) Least developed countries as defined by the United Nations.

16.5.4 The Foundation shall enforce patents against:

(a) Foreign entities seeking to exploit African innovations without fair compensation;

(b) Any entity using Foundation IP to undermine the Mission;

(c) Commercial entities refusing reasonable licensing terms.

16.6 Trade Secrets and Confidential Information

16.6.1 The Foundation shall maintain strict protections for trade secrets and confidential information, including:

(a) Proprietary algorithms and software architectures;

(b) Research data and methodologies;

(c) Strategic plans and acquisition targets;

(d) Security protocols (as provided in Annex M).

16.6.2 All employees, contractors, and partners with access to trade secrets shall sign confidentiality agreements with indefinite duration.

16.6.3 Breach of trade secret protection shall constitute gross misconduct under Article XXII and shall trigger immediate legal action.

16.7 Open Innovation and Open Source

16.7.1 Notwithstanding the protections above, the Foundation is committed to open innovation and open-source principles where they serve the Mission.

16.7.2 The Foundation shall:

(a) Publish non-core research data and findings for public benefit;

(b) Contribute to open-source software communities where consistent with the Mission;

(c) Share anonymized data for academic and policy research;

(d) Participate in continental and global standards-setting bodies.

16.7.3 The Foundation shall not publish or open-source:

(a) Core proprietary algorithms that give the Foundation competitive advantage;

(b) Security-related code or protocols;

(c) Trade secrets or strategic research.

16.8 Critical IP and the Research

16.8.1 Critical Intellectual property arising from the  Research Centers shall be owned by the Jowih IP Company but classified as sensitive.

16.8.2 Such IP shall not be:

(a) Published or open-sourced;

(b) Licensed to any foreign entity;

(c) Disclosed outside the Foundation without Steward Office approval.

16.9 Revenue and Mission Fund

16.9.1 All revenue generated from intellectual property - including licensing fees, royalties, and patent proceeds - shall flow to the Mission Fund (Article V.3).

16.9.2 No portion of IP revenue shall be distributed to any private person.

16.9.3 IP revenue shall be reinvested in research, development, and innovation capacity building.

16.10 Enforcement and Dispute Resolution

16.10.1 The Foundation shall pursue legal action against any entity infringing Foundation intellectual property rights.

16.10.2 The Foundation shall maintain a legal fund for IP enforcement, funded from the Mission Fund.

16.10.3 Disputes regarding IP ownership or licensing shall be resolved under Article XVIII (Dispute Resolution).

16.11 Annual IP Report

16.11.1 The Foundation shall publish an annual Intellectual Property Report, including:

(a) Patents filed and granted, by jurisdiction;

(b) Licensing agreements entered into;

(c) Technology transfer agreements executed;

(d) Revenue generated from IP;

(e) Legal enforcement actions taken.

16.11.2 The Annual IP Report shall be included in the Annual Impact Report (Article XXXV).

16.12 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 2 Core Article under Article XIX.3, requiring  approval for amendments to ensure the Foundation's intellectual property remains protected and mission-aligned in perpetuity.

Article XVII - Risk, Compliance & Insurance

17.1 Risk Management A formal Risk Register and Risk Management Policy shall be maintained; significant operational or reputational risks must be reported to the Board.  

17.2 Insurance Appropriate insurance (professional indemnity, directors & officers, property, medical) shall be maintained.  

Article XVIII - Dispute Resolution

18.1 Internal Resolution Disputes between Board Members, between Board and Founder, or between staff and Board shall first attempt internal resolution per procedures as in the ANNEX F.  

18.2 External Arbitration If internal resolution fails, disputes shall be settled by arbitration (e.g., Mauritius International Arbitration Centre) under laws of Mauritius or any other, unless parties agree otherwise.  

Article XIX - Amendment Procedure

Recital - Constitutional Integrity & Mission ContinuityThis Amendment Procedure exists to preserve the perpetual Mission of the Foundation, prevent hostile capture or mission drift, ensure intergenerational fidelity to Founding Purpose, and maintain global trust and legitimacy. Amendments shall only be permitted under exceptional conditions and through transparent, democratic, and expert-governed oversight.

19.1 Amendment Initiation

(1) An amendment to any Article of this Constitution may be proposed only by:(a) the Founder; or(b) a two-thirds (2/3) majority of the combined boards from Purpose Trust and Family Trust; or(c) a verified petition of at least five percent (5%) of registered active app users and verified citizens in the Governance OS referendum database submitted to the Board.

19.2 General Amendment Threshold (Non-Core Articles)

(1) Any amendment to a non-Core Article shall require:(a) a two-thirds (2/3) affirmative vote of the full combined boards from Purpose Trust and Family Trusts; and(b) approval by a three-quarters (3/4) majority of Members present and voting at a properly convened Extraordinary General Meeting; and(c) independent legal review confirming compliance with applicable law.

19.3 Core Articles - Tiered Entrenchment

19.3.1 Core Articles are divided into two Tiers based on their foundational importance to Mission protection and structural integrity:

Tier 1 Core Articles ("Sanctum")

These articles are given the highest level of protection to prevent mission drift.

(i) Article I - Definitions & Interpretation

(ii) Article II - Legal Form, Registered Office & Objects

(iii) Article III - Non-Profit & No-Equity Policy

(iv) Article V - Funding, Financial Controls & Revenue Flow

(v) Article V.A - Financial Disbursement Integrity & Anti-Leakage Mechanisms

(vi) Article VI - Founder & Founder Privileges

(vii) Article VII - Life Visionary Role & Limited Founder Powers

(viii) Article XIX - Amendment Procedure

(ix) Article XXI - Dissolution & Asset Distribution

(x) Article XXV - Asset Lock & Perpetuity

(xi) Article XXVI - Severability, Saving Clause & Anti-Diversion of Foundation Assets

(xii) Article XXVIII - Ownership & Control Structure (Mission Protection Framework)

(xiii) Article XXIX - Jowih Purpose Trust (Control Layer)

(xiv) Article XXX - Founder Family Trust (Legacy & Security Layer)

(xv) Article XXXI - Operating Companies and Dividend Flow

(xvi) Article XLVIII - Mission-Lock and Perpetual Alignment

Tier 2 Core Articles ("Critical Governance")

These articles are given extremely high level of protection to prevent mission drift.

(i) Article VIII - Board of Governors - Composition & Duties

(ii) Article XI - Responsible Staffing & Anti-Ghost Worker Safeguards

(iii) Article XIII - Direct Procurement, Execution & Strategic Partnerships

(iv) Article XIV - Safeguarding, Child Protection & Beneficiary Selection

(v) Article XXII - Ethics, Anti-Corruption & Sanctions

(vi) Article XXIII - Community Participation & Workforce Sovereignty

(vii) Article XXIV - Development Acceleration Framework

(viii) Article XXXIII - Mission Investment & Non-Voting Share Protections

(ix) Article XXXIV - Donations, Partnerships & Contributions

(x) Article XXXV - Transparency, Audit & Public Accountability

(xi) Article XXXVI - Whistleblower & Ethics Protection

(xii) Article XXXVII - Anti-Money Laundering & Counter-Terrorism Financing

(xiii) Article XXXVIII - Cultural Competence & Local Adaptation

(xiv) Article XXXIX - Technology & Data Integrity (Modern Compliance)

(xv) Article XLI - Investment and Endowment Management

(xvi) Article XLII - Monitoring, Evaluation, and Impact Assessment

(xvii) Article XLIII - Audit Supremacy & The Independent Auditor General

(xviii) Article XLIV - The Public Referendum Process

(xix) Article XLV - Strategic Acquisitions & Commercial Enterprise Alignment

(xx) Article XLVI - Emergency Response & Continuity of Operations

(xxi) Article XLVII - Political Neutrality & Advocacy Limits

(xxii) Article XLIX - Community-Driven Automation & Decentralized Governance

(xxiii) Article L - Distributed Infrastructure and Data Sovereignty

(xxiv) Article LI - Funding Integrity and Non-Dilution

(xxv) Article LIII - Phased Funding & Direct Disbursement Model

(xxvi) Article LIV - Africa Development Network (ADN)

(xxvii) Article LV - Incorporation of Private Annexes

19.3.2 Amendment Requirements for Core Articles

(1) Any amendment to a Core Article shall require:(a) Combined Purpose and Family Trusts Board Approval(i) Unanimous approval for Tier 1 Articles(ii) 75% supermajority approval for Tier 2 Articles(b) Founder's Consent - where living and competent(c) Public Referendum Approval (per Article XLIV)(i) 75% approval for Tier 1(ii) 66.7% approval for Tier 2(iii) Minimum 60% quorum of eligible voters(d) Independent Oversight Panel Approval - unanimous(e) Judicial Review(i) Not contrary to law or public policy(ii) In the long-term best interests of the Mission(iii) Free of improper influence

19.3.3 Streamlined Process for Non-Fundamental Tier 2 Amendments

(1) Applicability: Restricted to amendments that:(a) update references to law/technology/standards; or(b) clarify language without altering substantive rights; or(c) adjust timelines/thresholds/reporting where protections remain intact; and(d) are certified as non-fundamental by the Independent Auditor General and the Oversight Panel.

(2) Approval Pathway:(a) 85% Combined Trusts Board approval(b) Unanimous Oversight Panel approval(c) Written IAG certification(d) 90-day public notice prior to effect

(3) Exclusions: The following must not be modified in any circumstance:(a) The Dividend flow from Jowih Technologies and operating companies to the Holding Company and From Holding Company to the Foundations 90% / Family Trust 10%.(b) Jowih Purpose Trust powers(c) Founder Family Trust powers(d) Asset Lock(e) Amendment process itself

19.3.4 Mandatory Review of Tier 2 Articles

(1) Every ten (10) years the Oversight Panel shall review all Tier 2 Articles.(2) The review shall determine:(a) continuing relevance and effectiveness;(b) impact of legal/technological developments;(c) appropriate Tier classification.(3) Outcomes may include:(a) No change(b) Technical amendment (using 19.3.3)(c) Reclassification (requires Tier 2 amendment process)(4) Review does not itself constitute amendment.

19.4 Time-Lock

(1) No Core Article amendment becomes effective earlier than one-hundred-eighty (180) days after final approval.(2) Streamlined Tier 2 amendments remain subject to a ninety (90) day delay.

19.5 Structural Supremacy Clause

(1) The separation of control between:(a) the Jowih Purpose Trust (Article XXIX); and(b) the Foundation (Article II);is an integrated and legally enforceable system.

(2) Any attempt to amend a Core Article without strict compliance with this Article shall be null and void and shall:(a) constitute a fundamental breach of fiduciary duty; and(b) trigger enforcement mechanisms of the Jowih Purpose Trust.

19.6 Statutory Compliance & Existential Threat Safeguard

(1) Where strict legal compliance requires amendment to avoid:(a) dissolution; or(b) loss of core charitable status;the Board may approve the minimum change necessary by a 95% supermajority.(2) Such amendments shall be publicly disclosed with supporting legal opinions within seven (7) days.

19.7 Incorporation of Private Annexes

No amendment to this Constitution may alter, override, or invalidate the binding effect of the confidential ANNEXES A through L of the Private Constitution, except where such amendment explicitly addresses a specific ANNEX provision and complies with the amendment thresholds applicable to the related Core Article. Any inconsistency between this public Constitution and the Private Constitution shall be resolved in favor of this public Constitution, but the Private Constitution remains binding on all Foundation organs, trustees, officers, and volunteers as an operational and governance instrument.

19.8 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein.

ARTICLE XX - PARTNERSHIP WITH AFRICAN STATES & AFRICA UNION 

20.1 Support for National Goals

The Foundation exists to support and accelerate the achievement of continental and national development goals within its mission scope.The Foundation shall act as an execution partner, innovation accelerator, technology transfer conduit, and source of sustainable, non-conditioned catalytic funding for mutually agreed priorities.

The Foundation does not supplant the State. It strengthens the State's capacity to serve its citizens by providing capital, technical expertise, and implementation capability that states may lack.

20.2 Economic Benefit & Fiscal Multiplier

The Foundation recognizes that its projects generate local economic activity, employment, tax revenue, and fiscal stability. The Foundation shall:

(a) Structure projects to maximize local content, local labor, and local supply chains;

(b) Prioritize technology transfer and skills training as conditions of major infrastructure investments;

(c) Encourage co-investment models where states contribute land, regulatory facilitation, or policy alignment in exchange for Foundation capital and execution;

(d) Document and publish (in aggregate) the fiscal multiplier effect of Foundation projects to demonstrate the mutual benefit of partnership.

20.3 Oversight and Independence

The Foundation shall provide states with appropriate visibility into the impact and financial flow of its projects for accountability purposes. Such visibility shall include:

(a) Quarterly project progress reports;

(b) Audited financial statements for state-funded portions of projects (where applicable);

(c) Impact assessments verified by independent third parties.

However:

(d) Assets remain owned by the public through the Foundation, not political actors. No state may claim ownership, control, or disposition rights over Foundation assets.

(e) States have oversight visibility but not control over mission, operations, or user data. This ensures operational independence and mission integrity.

(f) No state may demand access to beneficiary data, whistleblower identities, or internal Foundation communications as a condition of partnership.

(g) The Foundation reserves the right to terminate any state partnership if the state demands conditions that violate the Foundation's Constitution, Core Articles, or ethical standards.

20.4 Non-Alignment & Non-Exclusivity

The Foundation shall not enter into exclusive partnership agreements with any single state that would prevent it from operating in other jurisdictions.

The Foundation shall not endorse, support, or oppose any political party, candidate, or faction within any country.

The Foundation shall not serve as an instrument of any state's foreign policy.

The Foundation shall not accept state funding that imposes political conditions inconsistent with Article XXXIV (Donations, Partnerships & Contributions).

20.5 No State Lending - Catalytic Funding Only

The Foundation does not issue loans to states. The Foundation is a development accelerator though its Mission Fund and Country Pots.

All Foundation funding to or through states shall be structured as:

(a) Grants - non-repayable funding for agreed projects;

(b) Challenge grants - performance-based funding released upon achievement of milestones;

(c) Co-investment - Foundation funds matched by state in-kind contributions (land, regulatory access, data, or local labor);

(d) Output-based disbursements - payment upon verified delivery of services or infrastructure.

The Foundation shall not:

(a) Issue loans to states - of any kind, under any structure;

(b) Charge interest on any state funding;

(c) Condition future funding on repayment of prior funding - because there is no repayment.

20.5.1 Sovereign Debt Purchase under Pillar I (Extraordinary Circumstances)

The Foundation may, in exceptional circumstances, purchase sovereign debt of selected African states solely for the purpose of cancellation - relieving the state of that debt burden.

Any such purchase shall require:

 Three-quarters approval of the Combined Trusts (Purpose Trust and Family Trust voting jointly), with no abstentions and no absentee permitted for any reason whatsoever. 

(b) Public disclosure of the purchase and cancellation within thirty (30) days of completion, including:

The state whose debt was purchased;

The face value of the debt;

The price paid by the Foundation;

Confirmation of cancellation.

The Foundation shall not:

(a) Purchase sovereign debt for investment or profit;

(b) Use sovereign debt as leverage to influence state policy;

(c) Trade sovereign debt on secondary markets;

(d) Hold sovereign debt as an asset - cancellation shall be immediate upon purchase, with proof of cancellation filed in the Foundation's public records.

Rationale: The Foundation exists to accelerate development, not to extract returns or create debt dependency. In rare cases, buying and canceling debt may serve the Mission under Pillar I (Empower Communities & Basic Needs) by relieving states of crushing debt burdens. In all cases, the Foundation must not become a coercive creditor.

20.6 Technology Transfer as Default Condition

For any infrastructure or technology project involving foreign suppliers, the Foundation shall require, as a default condition, a legally binding technology transfer agreement that:

(a) Provides African engineers and technicians with full access to relevant intellectual property (under license);

(b) Establishes local manufacturing, assembly, or maintenance capacity;

(c) Includes training and skills development components;

(d) Permits the Foundation to sub-license technology to African entities under fair terms.

Waivers to this requirement may be granted only by unanimous vote of the Combined Trusts and certification by the IAG that no alternative exists.

20.7 Dispute Resolution with States

Any dispute arising between the Foundation and a state shall first be subject to good faith negotiation. If unresolved within ninety (90) days, the dispute shall be submitted to binding international arbitration under the rules of:

(a) The Mauritius International Arbitration Centre (MIAC); or

(b) The Permanent Court of Arbitration (PCA) at The Hague; or

(c) Such other forum as mutually agreed.

The Foundation shall not submit to the jurisdiction of any national court that would subject it to political interference or unlawful data access.

20.8 Exit Strategy

The Foundation shall maintain, for each state of operation, a contingency exit plan that ensures:

(a) Beneficiary services continue uninterrupted;

(b) Foundation assets are protected from seizure;

(c) Data is evacuated or cryptographically shredded;

(d) Personnel are safely relocated.

The Foundation may activate its exit plan unilaterally if a state violates the terms of partnership or threatens the Foundation's mission integrity.

20.9 - ACCELERATED DEVELOPMENT FRAMEWORK AGREEMENT (ADFA)

20.9.1 Agreement with the State

The ADFA is a separate agreement document with the 54 African States as the legal entities i.e The Republic of Kenya and not political parties whether in government or opposition. The Foundation enters into the ADFA with the understanding that the State endures beyond elections, coups, revolutions, and constitutional reforms. The Foundation invests in nations.

20.9.2 Establishment

The Foundation may enter into an Accelerated Development Framework Agreement (ADFA) with any African state seeking to accelerate the implementation of The Great Africa Grid projects within its jurisdiction. The ADFA is a bilateral partnership agreement, negotiated in good faith, reflecting the mutual interests of both parties.Neither party shall act to undermine the spirit or intent of this Agreement while negotiations are ongoing.

20.9.3: Duration

The ADFA shall have an initial term milestone ending with the completion of the 2045 Horizon.

Thereafter, if agreed with host nation,the ADFA agreement shall automatically renew for successive twenty year horizons, to be known as Horizon 2065, Horizon 2085, and so on, matching The GAG’s long-term planning cycles by future generations.When agreed during ADFA agreement,renewal shall be automatic.

Either party may terminate only at the end of a full horizon, as per termination protocols. Termination shall not affect projects already funded or underway.

20.9.4 As core part of the Great African Grid ,The Foundation and signatory state may agree that the Foundation under The GAG shall execute projects directly in the following strategic sectors:

(a) Transportation infrastructure - Airports, seaports, railways, highways, bridges.

(b) Energy infrastructure - Power plants, national grids, transmission lines, renewable energy farms.

(c) Water infrastructure - Dams, treatment plants, distribution networks, irrigation systems.

(d) Digital infrastructure - National broadband backbone, data centers, satellite ground stations.

(e) Healthcare infrastructure - Regional hospitals, specialized clinics, medical research centers.

(f) Education infrastructure - University campuses, TVET centers, research laboratories.

(g) Housing and urban development - Mass affordable housing, slum redevelopment, smart city components.

(h) Research and innovation facilities - R&D hubs, technology parks, innovation centers.

20.9.5 The Foundation shall not compete with contractors for non-strategic infrastructure, routine maintenance, or non-strategic projects. The Foundation's direct execution is reserved for projects where global excellence ,speed, quality, and corruption-free delivery are essential to the Development.

20.9.6 The Foundation's direct execution is intended to:

(a) Demonstrate best practices in speed, quality, and Affordability;

(b) Transfer skills and technology to local contractors;

(c) Create a competitive benchmark that raises industry standards;

(d) Enable Near Free Development Acceleration to Host Nation while Assets remain public under Asset Locked never to be sold.

20.9.7 Infrastructure Delivery Models

20.9.8.1 The Foundation and signatory state may agree on the following delivery models for specific projects:

Model A (State-Funded) - Funding: 100% state budget. Ownership: 100% state. Foundation Role: Execution agent (at-cost).

Model B (Foundation-Funded) - Funding: 100% Foundation Mission Fund. Ownership: 100% Foundation (asset-locked). Foundation Role: Developer, operator, or lessor.

Model C (Co-Funded) - Funding: State X% plus Foundation Y%. Ownership: Proportional. Foundation Role: Co-developer, equity partner.

20.9.8 For Model A (State-Funded):

(a) The Foundation shall execute the project at cost, with no profit or markup.

(b) Any surplus shall be returned to the state or redirected to agreed project.

(c) The state owns the asset fully upon completion.

20.9.9 For Model B (Foundation-Funded):

(a) The Foundation shall fund, build, and own the asset.

(b) The asset remains owned by the public through the Foundation under Article XXV (Asset Lock).

(c) The Foundation shall not sell, transfer, or encumber the asset to any private party or foreign entity.

(d) The Foundation may operate the asset directly, lease it to a qualified operator, or transfer operation to the state.

(e) Any revenue generated from the asset shall flow to the Mission Fund.

20.9.10 For Model C (Co-Funded):

(a) Ownership shall be proportional to capital contribution.

(b) The Foundation's equity share is asset-locked under Article XXV and may not be sold to any private party or foreign entity.

(c) The Foundation may transfer its equity to the state as a grant at any time.

(d) The state may purchase the Foundation's equity at fair market value, with proceeds returning to the Mission Fund.

(e) Any revenue generated from the asset shall be distributed proportionally to ownership.

(f) The Foundation may purchase the State's equity at fair market value.

20.9.11 In no circumstance may the Foundation sell, transfer, or encumber any asset or equity to:

(a) Any private for-profit entity;

(b) Any foreign government or foreign state-owned enterprise;

(c) Any entity that would compromise the Foundation's mission or the Asset Lock.

20.9.12 Community Workforce Integration and Employment Creation

20.9.12.1 The Foundation shall integrate community workforce through the Comrade Hub network as set forth in Article XXIII.

20.9.12.2 Infrastructure projects executed under this ADFA shall serve as engines for employment creation, providing a direct pathway from community participation to certified skilled labor to permanent employment with the Foundation, its contractors, partners or its CDEs.

20.9.12.3 Contractors engaged on ADFA projects shall be required to hire certified Comrade Hub members for a minimum percentage of skilled positions, as determined by the Foundation.

20.9.12.4 Through this model, the ADFA and the Comrade Hub network shall systematically erode unemployment across participating states while building a sovereign, skilled, African workforce capable of operating and maintaining the continent's infrastructure.

20.9.12.5 The Foundation shall report annually on employment outcomes from ADFA projects as part of the workforce metrics under Article XXIII.7.

20.9.13 Continuity in the Event of Political Change or Extra-Constitutional Transition

20.9.13.1 Principle of Continuity

The ADFA is an agreement with the State as a sovereign legal entity and is not contingent upon any specific government, administration, or political authority.Accordingly, the rights and obligations arising under the ADFA shall continue irrespective of political transitions.

20.9.13.2 Non-Political Position of the Foundation

The Foundation shall remain strictly neutral in all internal political matters of any signatory state.It shall not:(a) Recognize, endorse, or oppose any political faction;(b) Intervene in governance disputes or political processes;(c) Participate in or influence outcomes of political transitions.

20.9.13.3 Continuity of Operations in Exceptional Circumstances

In the event of:

a coup d’état, an extra-constitutional change of government, or any form of irregular political transition, the Foundation shall:

(a) Continue its operations without interruption, to the extent reasonably practicable;(b) Maintain engagement with the de facto authorities exercising effective control, solely for the purposes of:

ensuring the safety of Foundation personnel and assets, and preserving the continuity of development operations;(c) Refrain from taking any position regarding the legitimacy of such authorities;(d) Expect that all authorities exercising control within the State shall honor the ADFA as a continuing obligation of the State. 

20.9.13.4 Successor Government Obligations

Any successor government, whether arising through constitutional or extra-constitutional means, shall be considered bound by the ADFA as an obligation of the State.

A successor government seeking to:terminate, or renegotiate the ADFA must do so strictly in accordance with the formal procedures established under this Agreement.

20.9.13.5 Termination and Renegotiation Protocol

(a) Any intention to terminate or renegotiate the ADFA shall require a minimum of twelve (12) months’ written notice;

(b) During the notice period: all ongoing projects shall continue unless otherwise mutually agreed; both parties shall engage in good faith discussions; 

(c) Unilateral repudiation of the ADFA without adherence to the prescribed notice and process shall constitute a material breach of the Agreement.

20.9.13.6 Multi-Stakeholder Continuity Engagement

For the sole purpose of safeguarding operational continuity, the Foundation may maintain non-political, technical lines of communication with relevant institutional and societal stakeholders within the State.

Such engagement shall:(a) Be strictly operational and non-political;(b) Not constitute recognition or endorsement of any faction;(c) Be limited to ensuring that the ADFA is understood as a State-level obligation and respected across transitions.

20.9.14 Accession

The ADFA shall be opened for signature by any African state. The Foundation shall maintain a public registry of signatory states on its Public Dashboard. A state may accede to the ADFA at any time during the 2045 Horizon.Notice that later accession would have less Accelerated development in that country and might be difficult to plan when other phases of the milestone have been completed in earlier ADFA signatory states.

The original text of the ADFA shall be deposited with the Secretary-General of the African Union and the Registrar of the Foundation. Certified copies shall be provided to each signatory state.

20.9.15 - Country pot augmentation by signatory states

20.9.15.1 Principle of Joint Contribution

Recognizing that accelerated development requires shared responsibility, ADFA signatory states may voluntarily contribute to their respective Country Pot to supplement the Foundation's allocation. Such contributions are optional and do not affect the Foundation's baseline funding commitment to the state.A state's contribution stays in its Country Pot. It is never moved to another country. It is never used for Foundation operations. It is never part of the Mission Fund. It is ring-fenced, locked, and deployed only within that state's jurisdiction.

20.9.15.2 Annual Contribution Agreement

Each year, the Foundation and the signatory state shall agree in writing on:

(a) The amount the state will contribute to the Country Pot for that fiscal year;

(b) The form of contribution (cash, in-kind, or a combination thereof);

(c) The allocation of state contributions between Model A infrastructure projects and CDE grassroots development;

(d) The specific projects or communities to be prioritized;

(e) The reporting and accountability framework for tracking utilization of state contributions.

20.9.15.3 Allocation of State Contributions

State contributions to the Country Pot shall be allocated as follows unless otherwise agreed in writing:

(a) Model A Projects: Up to 100% may be allocated to state-funded, Foundation-executed infrastructure projects where the state retains full ownership of the completed assets;

(b) CDE Development: Up to 100% may be allocated to community-led development projects disbursed through Community Development Entities (CDEs) in accordance with Article LIII;

(c) A combination of both, as agreed annually.

20.9.15.4 Execution and Accountability

(a) All funds contributed by the state to the Country Pot shall be held in the Foundation's ring-fenced Country Pot accounts;

(b) The Foundation shall execute all projects in accordance with its Constitution, regardless of funding source;

(c) The state shall receive quarterly reports on the utilization of its contributions, including project milestones, financial reconciliation, and impact metrics;

(d) All disbursements from the Country Pot shall follow the Direct Disbursement Model under Article LIII.3.1, with no funds passing through country-level intermediaries;

(e) The Independent Auditor General (IAG) shall audit all state contributions and their utilization as part of its ordinary audit cycle under Article XLIII.

20.9.15.5 No Effect on Baseline Funding

State contributions to the Country Pot shall not:

(a) Reduce the Foundation's baseline annual allocation to the Country Pot;

(b) Replace the Foundation's constitutional obligation to fund development projects under the ADFA;

(c) Create any entitlement to preferential treatment or accelerated timelines beyond what is agreed in the Annual Contribution Agreement.

20.9.15.6 No Coercion, Voluntary Contribution

No state shall be coerced, pressured, or conditioned to contribute to its Country Pot. All contributions are voluntary and are made at the state's sole discretion. The Foundation's baseline funding commitments shall remain unaffected by whether a state contributes or does not contribute.

20.9.15.7 Termination and Refund

(a) If a state contributes to its Country Pot and subsequently terminates the ADFA under Article 20.9.13, the Foundation shall refund any unutilized portion of the state's contributions within ninety (90) days of termination;

(b) Utilized funds shall not be refunded, as they have been applied to executed projects in accordance with the Annual Contribution Agreement.

20.9.15.8 Ring-Fence and Non-Diversion

(a) All funds contributed by a signatory state to its Country Pot shall remain exclusively within that state's Country Pot account.

(b) No funds contributed by a signatory state shall be:

(i) Transferred to any other state's Country Pot;

(ii) Used for Foundation administrative or operational expenses;

(iii) Added to the Mission Fund;

(iv) Deployed outside the contributing state's jurisdiction.

(c) Any attempt to divert, transfer, or utilize state contributions outside the contributing state's Country Pot shall constitute a Mission-Threatening Action under Article XLVIII.

(d) The Independent Auditor General (IAG) department shall certify quarterly that all state contributions remain within their ring-fenced Country Pot accounts.

20.9.16 Space Capability Partnership Framework
