The Foundation and signatory states recognize that African sovereignty requires independent access to space-based services, including Earth observation, communications, navigation, and security infrastructure. The Foundation shall establish a continental space capability framework in partnership with ADFA states and African private sector entities, pooling resources, expertise, and infrastructure to accelerate Africa's space capabilities. Participating states may contribute financial resources, land for ground stations and launch sites, regulatory facilitation, or in-kind support, receiving proportional equity stakes in resulting assets, data rights, and industrial benefits. The African private sector shall be the primary engine for design, manufacturing, and operations, with the Foundation providing catalytic capital, technical coordination, and program management. All assets developed under this framework shall be African-owned and asset-locked, with data stored and processed within African-controlled infrastructure. This framework shall be coordinated with the African Space Agency (AfSA) as the continental coordinating body, ensuring alignment with Agenda 2063 and existing African space programs.

20.9.17 Urbanization and Housing Framework

The Foundation and signatory states recognize that rapid urbanization and slum transformation are essential to achieving first world living standards across Africa. The Foundation shall establish a continental urbanization framework in partnership with ADFA states and African private sector entities, enabling the rapid deployment of dignified, asset-locked housing at scale. Participating states may contribute land where needed, regulatory facilitation, infrastructure connections, or in-kind support, receiving proportional benefits in the form of accelerated urbanization, job creation, and economic growth. The African private sector shall serve as the primary engine for construction, with the Foundation providing catalytic capital, escrow protection, and program coordination through the Housing deployment system. All housing developed under this framework shall be asset-locked under Article XXV of the Foundation's Constitution, with a dual-track model ensuring those who can invest may own while those who cannot rent affordably, with rent and mortgage payments reinvested into continuous scaling. This framework shall be executed in parallel across all signatory states, with each state's Country Pot and CDEs serving as the primary vehicle for community-led implementation.

20.9.22 Additional Development Frameworks

The Foundation and signatory states recognize that development needs extend beyond the specific frameworks enumerated in this Agreement. The Foundation and any signatory state may jointly adopt any additional development framework proposed by either party, covering any sector or thematic area consistent with the Foundation's Mission and the state's development priorities. Such frameworks shall follow the same principles of partnership, parallel execution, asset lock, and direct disbursement established in this Agreement. The Foundation shall provide catalytic capital, technical expertise, and program coordination for any adopted framework, while the state shall provide land, regulatory facilitation, and in-kind support where applicable. The African private sector shall be the primary engine for implementation, with the Foundation retaining operational control and mission protection under its Constitution. Any framework adopted under this Article shall be executed in parallel across participating states, with each state's Country Pot and CDEs serving as the primary vehicles for community-led implementation.

20.10 Tax Treatment

20.10.1 Principle

The Foundation, being a non-profit, asset-locked, mission-locked public benefit organization, shall seek tax-exempt status in all jurisdictions where it operates, consistent with its charitable purpose.

20.10.2 Mutual Recognition

The Foundation shall work with host states to establish mutually agreeable tax arrangements that:

(a) Recognize the Foundation's non-profit status;

(b) Avoid double taxation of Foundation activities;

(c) Facilitate the efficient deployment of Foundation resources to development projects;

(d) Comply with applicable tax treaties and international standards.

20.10.3 No Unilateral Demands

The Foundation shall not demand tax exemption as a condition of operation. Rather, it shall demonstrate its public benefit purpose and negotiate in good faith for appropriate tax treatment.

20.10.4 Reciprocity

Where a host state grants tax-exempt status to the Foundation, the Foundation shall commit to enhanced transparency and reporting on the use of resources within that jurisdiction.

20.11 Final Provisions

20.11.1 Severability

If any provision of this Article XX is held invalid or unenforceable, the remaining provisions shall remain in full force and effect.The separate ADFA agreement with host nation is an extension of this public constitution. This public constitution shall always supersede the ADFA agreement document where issues arise.

20.11.2 Amendment

This Article XX may be amended only under Article XIX tier 1 (Amendment Procedure).

20.12 Relationship with the African Union (AU)

20.12.1 Institutional Independence

The Foundation operates as an independent, non-political, and non-sovereign entity and is not established under, nor subordinate to, the institutional framework of the African Union (AU).

Nothing in this Agreement shall be interpreted as creating:(a) a subsidiary relationship;(b) an agency mandate; or(c) institutional subordination to the AU.

20.12.2 Respect for Continental Structures

The Foundation acknowledges the role of the African Union as a continental body representing its Member States.

All activities undertaken within AU Member States shall be conducted with due regard to:(a) regional cooperation objectives;(b) continental development frameworks; and(c) existing multilateral commitments of participating States.

20.12.3 Non-Interference with AU Authority

The Foundation shall not:(a) assume or replicate the governance functions of the AU;(b) interfere with AU institutional mandates; or(c) position itself as a replacement for continental coordination structures.

Its role remains strictly operational and execution-focused.

20.12.4 Independent State-Level Engagement

Engagement between the Foundation and any State shall occur on a bilateral basis through ADFA agreements.

Such engagement:(a) is independent of AU processes or approvals;(b) does not require authorization from the AU; and(c) shall not be affected by the State’s standing or position within the AU.

Participation by a State in this Framework remains a sovereign and voluntary decision.

20.12.5 Parallel and Complementary Access

The Framework operates in parallel with AU structures and does not replace or override them.

States shall retain simultaneous access to:(a) AU mechanisms and programs; and(b) Foundation-led initiatives

without conflict or exclusivity.

20.12.6 Cooperative Engagement (Non-Critical Domains)

Where appropriate, the Foundation may enter into cooperative arrangements with the African Union in clearly defined, non-critical domains for the purpose of:

(a) enhancing coordination;(b) avoiding duplication of efforts;(c) supporting regional integration objectives; and(d) improving execution efficiency at scale.

20.12.7 Nature of AU Cooperation

Any cooperation with the AU shall:

(a) be voluntary and mutually agreed;(b) be limited in scope to defined operational areas;(c) not extend to core execution authority of the Foundation;(d) preserve the Foundation’s independence in decision-making and implementation.

20.12.8 Safeguarding Direct State Relationships

No cooperative arrangement with the AU shall:(a) limit or condition direct engagement between the Foundation and participating States;(b) alter the legal standing of ADFA agreements; or(c) introduce additional layers of approval or control over bilateral operations.

20.12.9 Flexibility of Engagement

The Foundation retains discretion to:(a) expand, limit, or suspend cooperative arrangements with the AU;(b) determine the scope of such engagement;

based on operational requirements and alignment with its core mission.

20.13 Strategic Economic Implementation and Capacity Partnership Framework

20.13.1

The Foundation, acting through THE GAG, shall serve as a centre of economic and technical support, providing, upon request, advisory and implementation assistance to ADFA Signatory States. All engagements shall be cooperative, non-binding, and conducted with full respect for national sovereignty and independent economic decision-making.

20.13.2

The primary objective of this framework shall be to strengthen the practical capabilities of national institutions, particularly ministries responsible for finance, economic planning, and public resource management, enabling effective and self-reliant execution of state-defined economic policies.

20.13.3

Support provided under this framework shall focus on assisting Signatory States in operationalising their existing national strategies and regional commitments, including Agenda 2063 and AfCFTA, by translating established policy priorities into clear, actionable, and administratively feasible implementation pathways.

20.13.4

Where invited, the Foundation may deploy technical teams to work in partnership with relevant national institutions. These teams shall support the resolution of specific operational and administrative challenges, including but not limited to revenue systems, expenditure efficiency, regulatory processes, and public financial management, through practical, context-sensitive solutions.

20.13.5

Institutional strengthening shall be carried out through iterative, real-time application. Joint teams shall operate in defined review cycles, assessing the measurable impact of implemented adjustments, refining approaches based on observed outcomes, and supporting the gradual scaling of effective practices within national systems.

20.13.6

The Foundation shall maintain a secure and confidential knowledge system documenting proven administrative practices, implementation models, and policy execution methodologies derived from participating states. Structured exchange programmes shall be facilitated to enable peer learning and direct collaboration among officials of Signatory States.

20.13.7 Core Article Status

This  article is a Core tier 1 article Provision of the ADFA. No amendment shall remove or weaken the sovereign partnership principle, the invitation-only advisory model, or the step-by-step policy testing framework.

Article XXI - Dissolution & Asset Distribution

21.1 Dissolution Dissolution shall not occur except upon: a) A unanimous Combined Trusts Board resolution ; b) Approval by at least ninety percent (90%) of Members in a Referendum conducted among verified citizens in the Governance OS database and Members (with clearly published notice and a 60-day voting period, consistent with Article XIX); c) Adherence to the specific dissolution referendum procedure outlined in this Article and Article XLIV, which supersedes the general amendment procedure in Article XIX for this purpose; and d) Judicial approval in the registration jurisdiction confirming that dissolution is necessary (e.g., due to impossibility of fulfilling the Mission) and does not contravene public law, charitable purpose rules, or the Foundation's perpetual intent. The court may appoint an independent overseer to supervise the process. In alignment with the Foundation's establishment in perpetuity (Article XXV), dissolution shall only be considered in extraordinary circumstances, such as prolonged insolvency or legal mandate, and must preserve the Mission through asset transfer. At least 90% of eligible electorate must vote as stated in Article XLIV: The Public Referendum Process  .Dissolution  can be stopped immediately without any appeals by the public directly through the Golden Vote held in trust by the Founder Family Trust if the dissolution constitutes Mission Threatening Action and actors of the attempted Dissolution charged under jurisdiction laws.

21.2 Asset Distribution In the event of lawful dissolution, all assets shall be transferred only to another registered not-for-profit entity with substantially similar mission, objects, and commitment to asset lock provisions, as approved by the Combined Trusts Board, Members/Citizens/Verified app users (via referendum), and the court. The receiving entity must be verified by an independent auditor to ensure alignment. No assets or proceeds shall be distributed to private persons, Members, Governors, or affiliates. Any disputes regarding asset distribution shall be resolved through the procedures in Article XVIII (Dispute Resolution).  

21.3 No Private Residual Reiterated: No assets or proceeds shall be distributed to private persons. The Foundation's perpetual succession, akin to established humanitarian bodies, underscores that its existence and assets are dedicated indefinitely to public benefit.  

21.4 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein.

Article XXII - Ethics, Anti-Corruption & Sanctions

22.1 Preamble & Zero Tolerance Policy  The Foundation's mission and public trust are its most vital assets. This Article establishes an uncompromising zero-tolerance policy towards corruption, fraud, bribery, embezzlement, malfeasance, and any form of unethical conduct. This policy applies universally to all Directors,Executives Officers, Employees, Volunteers, Contractors, Agents, and Partners acting on the Foundation's behalf.  

22.2 Definitions of Prohibited Conduct  

a) Corruption: The abuse of entrusted power for private gain. This includes, but is not limited to, bribery, kickbacks, nepotism, cronyism, and extortion.  

b) Fraud: Any deliberate act of deception intended to secure an unfair or unlawful gain. This includes financial fraud (e.g., falsifying records, invoices, or expenses), misrepresentation of qualifications, and data manipulation.  

c) Malfeasance: The performance of an act that is legally forbidden or wholly wrongful. This includes willful and intentional wrongdoing that injures the Foundation's interests, reputation, or assets.  

d) Conflict of Interest: As detailed in Article XXVII, failure to disclose or manage a conflict of interest is a violation of this Article.  

22.3 Reporting Mechanisms & Protected Disclosure  

a) Multiple Reporting Channels: The Foundation shall maintain secure, accessible, and anonymous (where legally permissible) reporting channels, including:  

 i. A dedicated, confidential channel.  

 ii. An anonymous third-party hotline or digital platform.  

 iii. Direct reporting to the Foundation or the Independent Auditor General.  

b) Whistleblower Protection: Absolute protection from retaliation is guaranteed for any person who makes a good-faith report. Retaliation, including dismissal, demotion, harassment, or any form of discrimination, is itself a Category 1 violation and will result in severe sanctions.  

22.4 Internal Investigation Procedures  

a) Immediate Action: Upon receipt of a credible allegation, the CEO (if not implicated) or the Combined Trusts Board Chair and IAG(if not implicated) through its dedicated sub office handling such issues shall, within 48 hours, take steps to secure evidence and prevent further misconduct, which may include placing the subject(s) on paid administrative leave.  

b) Investigation Mandate: All investigations shall be conducted by the AD-HOC Ethics & Safeguarding Committee, overseen by the Independent Auditor General(if not implicated). For allegations against Steward offices, IAG,senior management or Board members, an independent external forensic with clean, reputable track record firm shall be engaged.  

c) Investigation Standards: Investigations must be thorough, impartial, public and documented through the Governance OS. Subjects of investigation have a right to be informed of the general nature of the allegations and to respond to evidence, subject to legal constraints and the integrity of the investigation.  

d) Investigative report can be used as additional evidence to be forwarded to host nation law authority where suspect is referred to host judicial processes.

22.5 Adjudication & Tiered Sanctions  The Combined Trusts Board, upon recommendation from the Ethics & Safeguarding Committee, shall determine the appropriate sanction based on the severity and nature of the violation.  

Category 1: Gross Misconduct (e.g.,Political move within Foundation, Theft, Embezzlement, Bribery, Major Fraud, Criminal Acts)  

Sanctions: Immediate dismissal, full restitution, permanent ban from any future association with the Foundation, and mandatory referral to law enforcement and regulatory bodies.  

 Legal Action: The Foundation will pursue all available civil and criminal remedies.  

Category 2: Serious Misconduct (e.g., Undisclosed major conflict of interest, falsification of records, harassment, retaliation)  

Sanctions: Dismissal or, in mitigating circumstances, final written warning, demotion, suspension without pay, and partial restitution.  

Consequence: Permanent ineligibility for promotion or appointment to a fiduciary role.  

Category 3: Minor Misconduct (e.g., Minor policy breaches, negligent (not fraudulent) misreporting)  

- Sanctions: Formal written warning, mandatory ethics training, and performance      monitoring.  

22.6 Restitution & Financial Recovery  

a) The Foundation shall take all necessary legal action to recover all losses, including misappropriated funds, legal costs, and investigative expenses, from the responsible individual(s).  

b) The "Asset Lock" (Article XXV) and "Anti-Diversion" (Article XXVI) clauses shall be invoked to trace and recover any Foundation assets.  

22.7 Public Reporting & Transparency  

a) Annual Ethics Report: The Foundation shall publish an Annual Ethics & Compliance Report, integrated into the Annual Impact Report.  

b) De-identified Reporting: This report shall summarize the number, nature, and outcome of all substantiated investigations, redacting personal identifiers to protect privacy, while ensuring transparency of process and consequence.  

c) Material Violations: Any Category 1 violation that has a material impact on the Foundation's finances or reputation shall be disclosed to the public and tier donors upon conclusion of the investigation and any ensuing legal proceedings.  

22.8 Third-Party Accountability  All contracts and partnership documents must include a clause requiring adherence to the Foundation's anti-corruption and ethics policies. Violation by a third party constitutes a material breach, granting the Foundation the right to immediately terminate the relationship and seek damages.  

22.9 Continuous Review  This Anti-Corruption Policy and the accompanying sanctions framework shall be reviewed annually by the ad-hoc Ethics & Safeguarding Committee to ensure it remains effective and aligned with international best practices.  

ARTICLE XXIII - COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION & WORKFORCE

23.1 Preamble: Principle of Co-Creation

The Foundation recognizes that sustainable impact is achieved not for communities, but with them. This Article institutionalizes the principle of co-creation, ensuring that the voices, wisdom, and agency of beneficiaries and local communities are central to the design, implementation, and evaluation of all Foundation programs.

23.2 Integrated Community Participation Framework

The Foundation shall maintain an integrated Community Participation Framework to ensure efficient, non-duplicative, and comprehensive community input. The components of this framework are:

(a) Beneficiary Advisory Councils (BACs): For deep, program-specific feedback from direct beneficiaries.

(b) Community Steering Assemblies (CSAs): For broader, regional-level consultation on impact, land use, and cultural matters.

(c) Development Assemblies (Article LIV.8): For country-level development and budget meetings and prioritization of national projects under the phased funding model (Article LIII).

(d) Comrade Hub: To channel innovation, talent, volunteer energy, and workforce development from all community members.

The CEO and PAN-Ambassadors are responsible for ensuring these mechanisms work in harmony, feeding insights upward without unnecessary repetition, as detailed in the Foundation's Operational Manual.

23.3 Authority and Integration into Governance

(a) The CEO shall include a standing "Community Voice" report in every Board meeting packet, summarizing input from all participatory structures and management's response.

(b) The Annual Impact Report (Article XXXV) shall include a dedicated chapter co-authored with community representatives, assessing the Foundation's performance against community-defined indicators of success.

(c) Community representatives, selected by their respective BACs and CSAs, shall be invited as non-voting participants in relevant Board Committee meetings (e.g., Ethics and Safeguarding, Program Oversight) to provide direct input.

23.4 The Comrade Hub: Definition and Scope

23.4.1 Definition of a Comrade

A "Comrade" means any citizen from the fifty four African nations participating in GAG’s initiative development activities and is registered into the Governance OS . A Comrade is recognized by their commitment to the Foundation's Mission, not by their educational or professional status. Comradeship is earned through participation, not conferred by title.

23.4.2 Structure and Governance

(a) Comrade Chapters: Citizen-led groups and all tertiary learning students.

(b) Entrepreneur Pods: African private sector enterprenours.

23.4.3 Strategic Functions and Integration

(a) Talent Pipeline and Recruitment: The Foundation shall give preferential consideration to qualified members of the Comrade Hub for internships, fellowships, and entry-level positions.

(b) Innovation Sourcing and R&D: The Foundation shall channel specific, well-defined challenges from its programs to the Comrade Hub network, inviting proposals and pilot projects. Jowih Venture Capital shall provide seed funding for the most promising ideas.

(c) Program Implementation and Scaling: Comrade Hub members may be engaged as localized implementation partners, leveraging their on-the-ground presence and expertise to scale the Foundation's impact efficiently.

(d) Policy and Advocacy: The network shall serve as a decentralized advocacy force, amplifying the Foundation's mission and the voices of its beneficiaries in public and academic forums.

23.5 Workforce Development and Career Pathway

The Foundation recognizes that Africa's greatest resource is its people, and that sustainable development requires not just infrastructure but a skilled workforce capable of building, operating, and maintaining all aspects of the Foundation's mission - from construction to healthcare, from education to technology, from research to administration.

The Comrade Hub shall serve as the Foundation's primary vehicle for workforce development across all sectors, evolving through three distinct phases over the 2045 Horizon.

23.5.1 Phase One - Exchange Programs & Voluntary Contribution (Years 1-3)

The Comrade Hub operates in parallel, not sequentially. While exchange programs send trainees abroad, local training continues at regional hubs and online.

Recruitment leads to Local Training leads to Certification leads to Placement into Track A or Track B.

The system does not wait for exchange graduates to return. Local training produces semi-skilled workers from Day 1. Exchange graduates return as trainers and supervisors, accelerating the pipeline.

In the early years of the Foundation, when the continent lacks sufficient skilled labor across multiple sectors and the Foundation's financial resources are being deployed to capital-intensive projects, the Comrade Hub from local training shall operate primarily on a voluntary basis.

This will be in place when the Foundation proceeds with its phase 1 of Exchange Programs to selected countries from year one to five.The exchange program is the primary vehicle for the Adapt strategy. Trainees sent abroad learn from global leaders and return with skills, relationships, and technology transfer agreements.

How Co-Development Ties to Exchange

When trainees are sent to a country or company, the Foundation negotiates a co-development or technology transfer agreement as a condition of the exchange. The partner gains access to African talent and markets. The Foundation gains access to technology, training, and joint intellectual property.

By the end of Year 5, 12,500 exchange program graduates have returned with global standard qualifications. These graduates become the core workforce for co-development projects.

Examples of Co-Development Tied to Exchange

North Africa Hub sends trainees to China for hydropower and desalination training. In return, Chinese partners agree to co-develop solar and desalination plants in Africa, with technology transfer and joint ownership of resulting intellectual property.

West Africa Hub sends trainees to India for digital infrastructure training. Indian partners agree to co-develop digital identity and payment systems adapted for African markets.

East Africa Hub sends trainees to China for rail and port training. Chinese partners agree to co-develop railway and port infrastructure with local manufacturing components.

Central Africa Hub sends trainees to Brazil for forestry management. Brazilian partners agree to co-develop sustainable forestry and mining technologies.

Southern Africa Hub sends trainees to Germany for manufacturing training. German partners agree to co-develop industrial manufacturing capacity with technology transfer.

Therefore;

(a) Volunteers shall contribute unskilled and semi-skilled labor to Foundation projects across all Pillars, including but not limited to:

(i) Pillar I (Empower Communities): Site clearing, manual material transport, basic trenching, landscaping, community liaison, quality monitoring, food distribution assistance, school feeding program support, vaccination campaign mobilization, elderly care assistance.

(ii) Pillar II (Digital Opportunity): Digital literacy training for community members, Jowih COS onboarding assistance, content moderation, translation services, data entry, basic IT support, youth mentorship.

(iii) Pillar III (Research and Innovation): Research participant recruitment, survey administration, laboratory assistance (basic), field data collection, sample processing assistance, open-source software testing.

(iv) Pillar IV (Future Civilization): Community outreach for new technologies, user experience testing, feedback collection, pilot program support, public awareness campaigns.

(v) Administration and Operations: Records organization, event coordination, translation services, reception and hospitality, logistics assistance.

 Volunteers shall receive:

(i) Reasonable stipends for transport, meals, and incidentals;

(ii) Safety equipment and training where applicable;

(iii) On-site meals where practicable;

(iv) Recognition and certification of participation;

(v) Priority access to Foundation training programs.

The Foundation's use of volunteer labor shall not displace paid workers, violate labor laws, or compromise safety or professional standards. Volunteer tasks shall be limited to those appropriate to their skill levels.

The Foundation estimates that volunteer labor during this phase shall reduce project costs across all sectors by twenty to forty percent (20-40%), allowing capital to be redirected to materials, equipment, specialized personnel, and scaling.

23.5.2 Phase Two - Skills Development and Certification (Years 3-6)

As Foundation projects scale and the Comrade Hub grows, the Foundation shall continue investing in structured training and certification programs for active Comrades.

(a) The skilled labor pool during this phase shall be enriched by multiple sources:

(i) Mega STEM University graduates - entering the Comrade Hub as skilled professionals, mentors, and trainers;

(ii) TVET graduates - certified tradespeople joining as skilled workers and trainers;

(iii) International exchange program participants - returning with new skills and perspectives;

(iv) Technology transfer beneficiaries - workers trained under Article 20.6 agreements;

(v) Comrades who have completed Phase One training - promoted from unskilled to semi-skilled or skilled status;

(vi) Professionals from other sectors - doctors, teachers, engineers, accountants, lawyers joining as skilled volunteers;

(vii) Diaspora professionals - Africans abroad contributing remotely or on short-term assignments.

(b) Training shall be delivered through:

(i) Mega STEM University extension programs;

(ii) Technical and Vocational Education and Training (TVET) partnerships;

(iii) On-project apprenticeships with skilled workers and engineers;

(iv) Technology transfer agreements with foreign partners (Article 20.6);

(v) Digital learning modules through the Jowih COS;

(vi) Professional certification programs in healthcare, education, technology, and management.

(c) Comrades who complete training and pass assessments shall receive:

(i) Formal certification recognized by the Foundation and its partners;

(ii) Registration in the Foundation's Skills Registry;

(iii) Priority consideration for paid positions on Foundation projects;

(iv) Assistance with job placement with contractors, CDEs, and partner organizations.

(d) During this phase, the Foundation shall begin transitioning certain roles from volunteer to paid status across all sectors, including:

(i) Equipment operators and maintenance technicians;

(ii) Skilled trades (welding, electrical, plumbing, carpentry, masonry);

(iii) Healthcare workers (community health workers, nursing assistants, lab technicians);

(iv) Teachers and trainers (literacy instructors, vocational trainers, digital skills facilitators);

(v) Quality control inspectors and safety officers;

(vi) Community training coordinators and supervisors;

(vii) Research assistants and data analysts;

(viii) IT support and software testers.

(e) The Foundation shall maintain a hybrid model during this phase: volunteer unskilled and semi-skilled labor alongside paid skilled labor, with the ratio shifting progressively toward paid positions as the Foundation's financial position strengthens.

23.5.3 Phase Three - Direct Employment (Year 6 and Beyond)

As the Foundation's financial position stabilizes and its project portfolio matures, the Foundation shall transition to a predominantly paid workforce model.

(a) The Foundation shall offer direct employment to certified Comrade Hub members for:

(i) Skilled construction and infrastructure roles;

(ii) Project supervision and management;

(iii) Training and capacity building;

(iv) Maintenance and operations;

(v) CDE enterprise management;

(vi) Healthcare delivery (clinics, hospitals, community health);

(vii) Education delivery (teaching, administration, curriculum development);

(viii) Research and laboratory roles;

(ix) Technology development and IT support;

(x) Administrative and professional roles;

(xi) Other roles as determined by the Foundation.

(b) Employment shall include:

(i) Competitive wages consistent with local market rates;

(ii) Safety protections and appropriate insurance;

(iii) Career progression pathways;

(iv) Access to further training and education;

(v) Where applicable, benefits as required by law.

(c) The Foundation shall not abandon the voluntary model entirely. A voluntary pool shall be maintained for:

(i) Community mobilization and liaison;

(ii) Short-term, high-intensity project support;

(iii) Emergency response and disaster relief;

(iv) Youth engagement and talent identification;

(v) Tasks where volunteer labor remains appropriate and cost-effective;

(vi) Professionals contributing pro bono services.

(d) The ratio of paid to volunteer labor on Foundation projects shall be reviewed annually by the foundation board and IAG and reported in the Annual Impact Report.

23.5.4 The Skills Registry

(a) The Foundation shall maintain a Skills Registry within the Governance OS, tracking each Comrade's:

(i) Participation history;

(ii) Training completed;

(iii) Certifications earned;

(iv) Skills assessments;

(v) Employment history with the Foundation or its partners.

(b) The Skills Registry shall be portable, allowing Comrades to present their credentials to other employers.

(c) The Skills Registry shall be anonymized for research purposes, allowing the Foundation to track workforce development outcomes across all sectors.

23.5.5 Contractor and Partner Requirements

(a) All contractors engaged on Foundation-funded projects shall be required to:

(i) Hire a minimum percentage of certified Comrade Hub members for skilled positions, as determined by the Foundation and adjusted annually;

(ii) Provide on-the-job training to Comrade Hub members assigned to their projects;

(iii) Report hiring and training data to the Foundation for inclusion in the Skills Registry.

(b) Contractors that demonstrate excellence in Comrade Hub hiring and training shall receive preference in future Foundation procurement.

(c) Partner organizations receiving Foundation support shall be encouraged to adopt similar practices.

23.5.6 CDE Employment Integration

(a) The Foundation's Community Development Enterprise (CDE) network (Article LIII.2.2) shall serve as an employment pipeline for certified Comrade Hub members, particularly in rural areas.

(b) Each CDE shall be encouraged to:

(i) Hire certified Comrades for its business ventures;

(ii) Provide further training and advancement opportunities;

(iii) Report employment data to the Foundation.

23.5.7 Professional Comrade Corps

(a) The Foundation recognizes that skilled professionals - doctors, engineers, teachers, accountants, lawyers, researchers - may wish to contribute their expertise on a voluntary or reduced-compensation basis.

(b) The Foundation shall establish a Professional Comrade Corps within the Comrade Hub, allowing skilled professionals to:

(i) Contribute pro bono or reduced-rate services to Foundation projects;

(ii) Mentor younger Comrades;

(iii) Provide technical assistance to CDEs and community enterprises;

(iv) Serve on advisory panels and technical working groups.

(c) Professional Comrades shall receive recognition, networking opportunities, and priority consideration for paid consulting or employment roles.

23.5.8 The Strategic Logic of Progressive Workforce Development

(a) This progressive workforce model is not charity. It is strategic.

(b) In the voluntary phase, the Foundation benefits from twenty to forty percent cost reduction across all sectors, while Comrades receive Exchange programs, local training,receive stipends, meals, experience, and training access.

(c) In the skills development phase, the Continent gains a skilled workforce for future projects from multiple sources (Mega STEM University, TVET, exchange programs, technology transfer, and Phase One graduates), while Comrades earn certifications and job readiness.

(d) In the employment phase, the Continent secures a permanent, loyal, skilled workforce across all sectors, while Comrades receive salaries, careers, and economic mobility.

(e) The Foundation does not exploit volunteer labor. It invests in human capital, knowing that today's volunteer carrying bricks is tomorrow's certified equipment operator, and tomorrow's supervisor, and tomorrow's contractor. And that today's medical student volunteering at a Foundation clinic is tomorrow's chief of medicine at a Foundation hospital.

(f) This model aligns with the Foundation's core mission: to accelerate development while building the capacity of Africans to lead that development themselves.

23.6 Protection of Participants

All community and beneficiary participants shall be covered by the Foundation's insurance and safeguarding policies. They shall not be exposed to harm, and their participation shall be based on informed consent. Reasonable stipends or reimbursements for their time and expenses shall be provided to ensure equitable access to participation, particularly for those from marginalized backgrounds.

23.7 Review and Evaluation

(a) The effectiveness of these participatory mechanisms and the Comrade Hub partnership shall be evaluated every three years by an independent body. The findings shall be made public and used to continuously strengthen the Foundation's commitment to community-led development.

(b) The Foundation shall track and report annually on:

(i) The number of Comrade Hub volunteers;

(ii) The number of Comrades trained and certified;

(iii) The number of Comrades employed by the Foundation, contractors, and CDEs;

(iv) The total wages paid to former volunteers;

(v) The cost savings achieved through volunteer labor;

(vi) Workforce development outcomes.

(c) These metrics shall be included in the Annual Impact Report (Article XXXV).

23.8 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 2 Core Article under Article XIX.3, requiring supermajority approval for amendments to ensure the Foundation's commitment to community participation and workforce development cannot be diluted.

ARTICLE XXIV - DEVELOPMENT ACCELERATION FRAMEWORK

24.1 Principles of Accelerated ExecutionThe Foundation shall implement its Mission and Four Strategic Pillars through an integrated acceleration framework designed to achieve compound impact toward the 2045 Horizon. This framework combines centralized model development with decentralized execution, ensuring systematic progression from foundational stabilization to technological leadership across all African nations.

24.2 Centralized Model Development & ReplicationThe Foundation shall establish and maintain centralized flagship projects as continental centers of excellence. These institutions/companies shall serve as primary development and innovation engines, creating proven models for systematic replication. The Foundation shall accelerate model development through concurrent engineering, modular design principles, and technology-enabled work flows that compress traditional development timelines.

24.3 Decentralized Country Implementation System(Dual Track Acceleration)Upon automatic transition to the Dual-Track Acceleration Phase under Article LIII.1.4, the Foundation shall initiate parallel country-level implementation through decentralized budget allocations. Country projects shall systematically adopt and adapt flagship models through structured replication protocols, maintaining fidelity to core standards while allowing for local contextualization. During early implementation stages, country-level initiatives shall prioritize Pillar I (Empower Communities & Basic Needs) and Pillar II (Digital Opportunity & Youth Advancement) with initial allocations of seventy percent (70%) to Pillar I and twenty-five percent (25%) to Pillar II of country budgets, and five percent (5%) for selective Pillar III and IV initiatives based on local capacity.During late stages approximately year ten (10) and above the country level shall heavily prioritize pillar III and IV.

24.4 Progressive Sector DevelopmentThe Continental Development Assembly (CDA) shall conduct annual reviews of country implementation progress. Progression toward balanced sector development across all Four Pillars shall occur automatically based on achievement of capacity milestones verified by the Independent Auditor General. This progressive reallocation shall advance toward the 2045 Horizon objective of continental technological leadership and civilizational advancement, ensuring all sectors are progressively lifted as foundational stability is achieved. The Foundation Board's role is limited to approving the milestone framework; it cannot unilaterally slow or prevent the reallocation of resources once milestones are certified.

24.5 Integrated Acceleration MethodologiesThe Foundation shall employ the following acceleration methodologies across all operations:a) Community Co-Investment Model: The Foundation shall formally partner with community organizations, local governments, and volunteer networks to mobilize voluntary labor, local materials, and traditional knowledge, reducing implementation costs while building sustainable local ownership.b) Digital-First Service Delivery: The Foundation shall leverage its Jowih COS and digital platforms as primary service delivery channels for education, healthcare information, financial inclusion, and skills training, achieving scale through technology multiplication effects and governance through the Governance OS.c) Modular Implementation Architecture: All Foundation interventions shall be designed as interoperable modules that can be deployed independently or in integrated systems, enabling rapid adaptation and scaling across diverse contexts.d) Strategic Partnership Leverage: The Foundation shall accelerate capability development through formal partnerships with national governments, specialized reputable non-governmental organizations, academic institutions, and private sector entities, avoiding redundant capacity building while accessing world-class expertise where needed.

24.6 Pillar-Specific Acceleration Mandates24.6.1 Pillar I Acceleration: The Foundation shall accelerate basic needs provision through integrated service hubs that cluster essential services, community construction programs that train and equip local volunteers, and progressive infrastructure enhancement that begins with functional minimum viable products, with priority given to modular housing, renewable energy micro-grids, smart health clinics, and clean water systems as specified in Article LIII.

24.6.2 Pillar II Acceleration: The Foundation shall accelerate digital opportunity through platform-scale education delivery, tiered skill development pathways that enable continuous advancement, and practice-to-theory learning approaches that use real community challenges as training grounds, focusing on digital literacy, vocational training, youth scholarships, and local Jowih COSdeployment as defined in Article LIII.

24.6.3 Pillar III Acceleration: The Foundation shall accelerate research and innovation through problem-focused research priorities, open innovation platforms accessible to multiple institutions, and applied research pipelines that systematically convert findings into field implementations, with selective country-level adoption based on demonstrated local capacity and alignment with flagship research hubs.

24.6.4 Pillar IV Acceleration: The Foundation shall accelerate advanced technology development through strategic capability partnerships, infrastructure layering that builds on established foundations, and African-centric design principles that prioritize local contexts and needs, with country implementations limited to demonstration projects and capacity-building initiatives until sufficient local expertise is developed.

24.7 Cross-Pillar Integration ProtocolThe Foundation shall ensure systematic integration across all Four Pillars through:a) Structured talent mobility pathways that enable movement between pillar-specific initiatives;b) Shared learning systems that capture and disseminate successful approaches across domains;c) Compound benefit design that structures interventions to generate multiple pillar benefits simultaneously.

24.8 Implementation Velocity FrameworkThe Foundation shall maintain implementation velocity through:a) Rapid prototyping cycles that test new approaches through small-scale pilots with clear success metrics;b) Parallel execution models that enable simultaneous deployment across multiple countries and contexts;c) Adaptive management protocols that empower field teams to make localized adjustments while maintaining strategic alignment.

24.9 Monitoring & Evolution Toward The HorizonsThe Foundation shall continuously monitor acceleration effectiveness through real-time performance tracking, quarterly learning cycles, and annual strategy reviews. The Continental Development Assembly (CDA) shall conduct comprehensive biennial reviews of the acceleration framework, assessing progress toward the Horizonsnobjectives and recommending strategic adjustments to pillar prioritization, funding allocations, and implementation methodologies to ensure all sectors achieve the targeted developmental milestones. The Foundation Board shall approve these recommendations unless the Independent Auditor General certifies they would constitute a Mission-Threatening Action.

24.10 Core Article StatusThis Article is designated as a Tier 2 Core Article under Article XIX.3, requiring supermajority approval for amendments to ensure perpetual commitment to accelerated development execution toward the 2045 Horizon.

Article XXV - Asset Lock & Perpetuity

25.1 Preamble: The Principle of Irrevocable Dedication  

The assets of the Foundation are not its own to own, but to hold in sacred trust for the fulfillment of its Mission and the benefit of its beneficiaries, both present and future generations . This Article establishes an absolute and permanent barrier against the diversion, privatization, or diminution of these assets, ensuring they are dedicated irrevocably to their charitable purpose.  

25.2 Definition of "Assets"  The term "Assets" shall be interpreted in its broadest possible sense and includes, but is not limited to:  

a) Financial Assets: All cash,jowih credits, bank deposits, investments, stocks, bonds, digital currencies, and any other financial instruments.  

b) Physical Assets: All land, buildings, vehicles, equipment, infrastructure, and any other tangible property.  

c) Intellectual Property: All copyrights, trademarks, patents, trade secrets, software code, brand identities, and the "Jowih" name and Jowih COS platform/Operational companies.  

d) Equity & Economic Rights: All shares, membership interests, dividend rights, and any other ownership or economic interests in any Operating Company or affiliated entity, as defined in Articles XXIX, XXX, and XXXI.  

e) Future Assets: Any and all assets of any kind acquired by the Foundation in the future, regardless of source (donations, revenue, capital gains, etc.).  

25.3 The Absolute Asset Lock  

a) No Private Inurement: No part of the income, earnings, or assets of the Foundation shall ever inure to the benefit of, or be distributed to, its Members, Directors, Officers, employees, or any other private persons, except as direct and reasonable compensation for services actually rendered or as permitted under a Mission Investment Agreement (Article XXXIII).  

b) Prohibition on Encumbrance: No Foundation asset shall be pledged, mortgaged, or used as collateral for any debt or obligation that does not directly and exclusively serve the Mission, and only then with prior unanimous Board approval and a public declaration of purpose.  

c) No Mission-Diverting Sales: The Foundation shall not sell, lease, or otherwise dispose of its core mission-critical assets (e.g., orphanage facilities, endowment principal) except in the furtherance of the Mission. Any such transaction must demonstrably enhance the Foundation's charitable capacity and is subject to the referendum process for Core Articles (Article XIX).  

d) Anti-Diversion Integration: Any act in violation of Article XXVI (Anti-Diversion of Foundation Assets) shall be treated as a fundamental breach of this Asset Lock.  

25.4 Permitted Uses and Transfers  Notwithstanding the absolute lock, assets may be applied or transferred only as follows:  

a) For Direct Mission Fulfillment: Expenditures directly for the programs, operations, and capital projects that advance the Objects outlined in Article II.  

b) To Mission-Aligned Entities: Upon lawful dissolution or as part of a strategic reorganization, assets may be transferred only to another non-profit organization, foundation, or charitable trust that:  

 i. Has objects substantially similar to those of this Foundation.  

 ii. Is bound by an equally robust and legally enforceable asset lock clause in its governing documents.  

 iii. Is approved by a Member/public referendum and receiving a confirming judicial opinion, as per the dissolution procedure in Article XXI.  

c) For Mission-Aligned Investments: Assets may be invested in accordance with the Mission-Aligned Investment Policy (Article XLI), provided such investments do not contradict the Foundation's core values and the primary purpose remains the generation of returns for the Mission, not speculative gain.  

25.5 Structural Reinforcement of the Asset Lock  The Asset Lock is not merely a clause but a structural reality, enforced through:  

a) The Jowih Purpose Trust (Article XXIX): Which holds 60% voting control over Operating Companies to prevent any corporate action that could compromise the Foundation's assets or the 90% dividend flow at the Holding Company.  

b) The Founder Family Trust (Article XXX): Which is itself bound by the ethical and anti-diversion rules of this Constitution, preventing the 10% economic interest and 40% voting rights from being used to undermine the Mission.  

c) The Amendment Barrier (Article XIX): Which designates this Article as a Core Article, making it extremely difficult to amend and protecting it from future dilution or repeal.  

25.6 Perpetuity: The Enduring Covenant  

a) Declaration of Perpetual Existence: The Foundation is established as a perpetual succession, intended to exist forever, transcending the lives of its Founder, its current Board, and its present beneficiaries. Its existence is dedicated indefinitely to public benefit.  

b) Inter-Generational Equity: The Foundation's investment and spending policies shall be managed with inter-generational equity in mind. The needs of current beneficiaries shall be met without compromising the ability of future generations to benefit from the Foundation's endowment and mission-critical assets.  

c) Defense Against Dissolution: Dissolution of the Foundation is an extraordinary measure, permitted only under the most stringent conditions as detailed in Article XXI, and must always result in the transfer of all assets to another perpetual, mission-locked entity.  

25.7 Enforcement and Consequences of Breach  

a) Ultra Vires Act: Any transaction, contract, or decision that violates this Asset Lock shall be deemed ultra vires (beyond the power) of the Foundation and void ab initio (from the outset).  

b) Personal Liability: Any Director, Officer, or agent who knowingly authorizes, facilitates, or participates in a breach of this Asset Lock shall be personally and jointly liable for the full restoration of the lost assets to the Foundation, plus all associated legal and recovery costs.  

c) Standing to Sue: In addition to the Board, any Member of the Foundation, or the Independent Auditor General, shall have the legal standing to bring a derivative lawsuit on behalf of the Foundation to enforce this Article and recover misappropriated assets.  

d) Criminal Referral: Willful and malicious violation of this Asset Lock shall be reported to the relevant authorities for investigation as criminal theft, fraud, or breach of trust.  

25.8 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein.

ARTICLE XXVI - SEVERABILITY, SAVING CLAUSE & ANTI-DIVERSION OF FOUNDATION ASSETS

26.0 Severability and Saving Clause If any provision of this Constitution is found to be unenforceable under applicable law, such invalidity shall not affect the remaining provisions, which shall remain in full force and effect. Any invalid provision shall be replaced by a lawful provision that most closely reflects the economic and moral intent of the original.  

26.1 Prohibition of Private Diversion No Director, Officer, Employee, Consultant, or Affiliate of the Foundation shall, directly or indirectly, use, commit, lend, or transfer any Foundation asset, fund, intellectual property, or brand for the purpose of establishing, financing, or benefiting any private enterprise, company, or venture that is not expressly approved as a mission-aligned entity under Article XXXI.  

26.2 Foundation Ownership Requirement Any new enterprise, subsidiary, or spin-off utilizing Foundation resources, personnel, or intellectual property must be legally constituted as a Foundation-Linked Entity and shall adopt the ownership structure described in Articles XXIX, XXX, and XXXI. Any deviation shall be void ab initio and treated as a misappropriation of Foundation assets.  

26.3 Breach and Remedies Any violation of this Article constitutes gross misconduct and a breach of fiduciary duty. The Combined Trusts Board shall take immediate recovery action, including but not limited to restitution, dismissal, civil recovery, and, where applicable, criminal referral. The Oversight Panel shall supervise investigation and ensure full restitution to the Foundation.  

26.4 Disclosure and Accountability All new entities proposed by any Officer, Director, or Affiliate must be disclosed in writing to the Foundation Board and recorded in the Foundation Register of Interests prior to any engagement or funding. Undisclosed or unauthorized ventures are deemed conflicts of interest and subject to sanctions under Article XXVII (Conflict of Interest). 

 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein. 

Article XXVII - Conflict of Interest

27.1 General Duty of Loyalty Every Steward President,trustees, CEO, Director, Officer, Employee, and Affiliate of the Foundation owes a fiduciary duty of loyalty to the Foundation ,its Mission and this constitution and shall act honestly, transparently, and in the best interests of the Foundation’s Mission as set out in Article II. No person shall place personal, familial, or financial interests above the Foundation’s objectives.  

27.2 Definition of Conflict of Interest A “Conflict of Interest” arises when an individual’s personal interests,politics, relationships, or financial activities could improperly influence, or appear to influence, the performance of their duties for the Foundation.  

Conflicts include, but are not limited to:  

a) Holding a financial interest in any organization doing business with the Foundation;  

b) Receiving personal benefit (monetary or non-monetary) from Foundation decisions or contracts;  

c) Using Foundation assets, information, or opportunities for personal advantage;  

d) Engaging in activities that compete with or undermine the Foundation’s Mission or integrity;  

e) Involvement of immediate family members, close associates, or related entities in Foundation transactions.  

27.3 Duty of Disclosure  

a) Every trustee,Director, Officer, or Employee must disclose in writing any actual, potential, or perceived conflict to the Foundation Board immediately upon awareness.  

b) The disclosure must specify the nature, extent, and potential impact of the conflict.  

c) Disclosures shall be entered into the Foundation Register of Interests, maintained by the Secretary, and reviewed annually by the Oversight Panel.  

27.4 Recusal and Abstention  

a) Any person with a disclosed conflict shall recuse themselves from participating in deliberations, decisions, or votes related to the matter.  

b) The minutes of the meeting shall record the disclosure and recusal explicitly.  

c) No conflicted person shall attempt to influence others in any decision where they have a conflict.  

27.5 Oversight Panel Review  

a) The Oversight Panel shall independently review all conflict disclosures, determine whether a conflict exists, and prescribe remedial action.  

b) The Panel may seek external legal or audit opinions if necessary.  

c) Serious or unresolved conflicts shall be reported to the full Combined Trusts Board for resolution, and if material, disclosed publicly in the Foundation’s Annual Impact Report.  

27.6 Prohibited Acts The following are strictly prohibited and constitute gross misconduct:  

a) Concealing or failing to disclose a conflict;  

b) Diverting Foundation funds, opportunities, or intellectual property for personal or third-party gain;  

c) Participating in decisions that result in personal or related-party benefit;  

d) Using Foundation information obtained in confidence for personal advantage after leaving office.  

e) Using Foundation assets to acquire companies without following the rigorous procedures and restructuring requirements of Article XLV.  

27.7 Sanctions and Remedies  

a) Violation of this Article shall constitute a breach of fiduciary duty and grounds for disciplinary action, removal, restitution, and, where applicable, civil or criminal referral.  

b) The Board shall recover any unjust enrichment, loss, or benefit obtained through violation of this Article.  

c) The Oversight Panel shall ensure that remedial and legal actions are executed promptly.  

27.8 Periodic Certification  

a) All trustees,Directors, CEOs, Officers, and key Employees shall annually sign a Conflict of Interest Declaration Form affirming that they have disclosed all relevant interests and understand their ongoing duty of loyalty.  

b) Failure to sign or update the declaration constitutes a compliance breach.  

27.9 Transparency and Public Accountability The Foundation shall include in its Annual Impact Report a section summarizing:  

· The number of conflict disclosures received and resolved;  

· Actions taken to enforce compliance;  

· Measures to strengthen ethical governance.  

Names of individuals shall not be disclosed, but transparency on procedures shall be maintained.  

27.10 Cross-Reference to Other Articles  

a) Any breach of this Article shall trigger the procedures under Article XXVI (Anti-Diversion of Foundation Assets).  

b) No person under investigation for a conflict shall vote or act on any matter concerning their role or remuneration until the matter is resolved.  

ARTICLE XXVIII - OWNERSHIP & CONTROL STRUCTURE

28.1 Integrated Holding Structure  

The Foundation shall operate through a unified ownership structure designed to permanently separate mission control from economic benefits while ensuring operational efficiency and perpetual mission alignment. This structure comprises four interdependent layers:

a) Jowih Purpose Trust(Article XXIX) as the perpetual mission guardian;

b) Founder Family Trust (Article XXX) as the Founder legacy and economic security layer with Golden Vote protection in Mission threatening actions only ;

c) Jowih Holdings Limited (Mauritius) as the central operating and ownership entity;

d) Jowih Group: Operations and Execution of the companies and the GAG initiative under the Holding Company board

d) Operating Subsidiaries (Article XXXI) as mission-aligned economic engines i.e Jowih IP company, Jowih Technologies Company.

28.2 Rights ,Ownership Allocation  

a) Jowih Holdings Limited It is fully owned by the Foundation and co managed by the Purpose Trust 60% voting right and Founder Trust 40% voting rights;The purpose Trust holds no direct economic rights as it only ensure that the 90% economic rights of the Foundation are received to the Mission Fund, the Founder Family Trust holds the rest 10% economic right for financial security.

b) The Jowih Group is not a separate legal entity but an organizational and operational designation for coordination, execution, and continental integration of all commercial activities under the Foundation’s mandate.

b) All Operating Subsidiaries (including the jowih technology Company, Jowih CRT.Spin Offs,Acquired companies ,IP Company, Logistics, Payments, Media, Gaming, R&D Centers, Innovation Support, and future ventures) shall be 100% owned by Jowih Holdings Limited;

c) Foundation Assets remain 100% under Foundation control, receiving defined dividend flows for mission fulfillment;

d) IP Ownership: All intellectual property shall be held by Jowih IP Holdings (Singapore), a 100%-owned subsidiary of Jowih Holdings Limited.

28.3 Structural Safeguards  

a) Mission Primacy: The Jowih Purpose Trust's 60% voting ownership and Founder Trust 40% voting ownership of the Jowih Holding Company ensures strategic, balanced and perpetual mission alignment across all subsidiaries;

b) Economic Fairness: The Foundation’s 90% economic share & Founder Family Trust's 10% economic interest is constitutionally protected while prevented from mission interference;

c) Operational Centralization: All commercial activities flow through Jowih Holdings Limited for governance consistency and strategic alignment;

d) Asset Protection: All structures are subject to the Asset Lock (Article XXV) and Anti-Diversion rules (Article XXVI).

ARTICLE 28.4 - JOWIH HOLDINGS COMPANY

28.4.1 Establishment and Role

a) The Foundation shall establish Jowih Holdings Limited (the “Holding Company”) as the central commercial and operational execution vehicle of the Jowih system.

b) The Holding Company shall be responsible for:i. Executing all commercial, infrastructure, and operational components of the Foundation’s mission;ii. Owning, controlling, and coordinating all Operating Subsidiaries;iii. Implementing integrated systems across all sectors and jurisdictions.

c) The Holding Company shall be guided under private ANNEX P

28.4.2 Legal Nature

a) The Holding Company shall operate as a separate legal entity, distinct from the Foundation and associated Trust structures.

b) It shall function on a commercial and financially sustainable basis, while remaining fully bound by:i. The mission and principles of the Foundation;ii. Governance controls established under this Constitution;iii. Oversight exercised through the Trust Structure.

28.4.3 Ownership, Economic Rights, and Control

a) The economic rights of the Holding Company shall be structured as follows:i. The Jowih Foundation shall hold ninety percent (90%) of the economic rights where dividends got to Mission fund in Foundation Treasury;ii. The Founder Family Trust shall hold ten percent (10%) of the economic rights for financial security .

Economic rights shall not confer voting or governance rights unless explicitly stated.

b) Governance, control, and oversight shall be exercised through the Trust Structure, including:i. The Jowih Purpose Trust;ii. The Founder Family Trust.

c) Voting rights shall be structured as follows:i. Jowih Purpose Trust - sixty percent (60%) voting rights;ii. Founder Family Trust - forty percent (40%) voting rights.

The division is to ensure that power is not concentrated and that every majority vote must be from both trusts working together to achieve majority voting in decisions made. 

d) The Founder Family Trust shall retain a Golden Vote on all matters deemed mission-critical in accordance with Article XXX.4. Golden vote may be activated by  any single trustee to ensure no point of failure.

28.4.4 Board Composition and Leadership

a) The Holding Company Board shall comprise nine (9) Directors:i. Four (4) appointed by the Jowih Purpose Trust;ii. Three (3) appointed by the Founder Family Trust;iii. Two (2) Independent Directors jointly approved by both Trusts.

b) Independent Directors shall:i. Possess proven expertise in governance, finance, infrastructure, or technology;ii. Have no material affiliation with either Trust.

c) The Chairperson shall:i. Be selected unanimously from among the Independent Directors;ii. Not hold any executive position within the Jowih Group.

28.4.5 Powers and Authority of the Board

a) The Board shall have ultimate authority over the Holding Company,Jowih Group and all subsidiaries, including:i. Strategic direction and expansion;ii. Capital allocation and financing decisions;iii. Approval of major transactions;iv. Oversight of subsidiary performance.

b) This authority shall be subject only to:i. The Golden Vote provisions;ii. Mission protection mechanisms established under this Constitution.

28.4.6 Executive Management

a) The Board shall appoint, oversee, and if necessary remove:i. The Group Chief Executive Officer (Group CEO);ii. Chief Executive Officers of all Operating Subsidiaries.

b) The Group CEO shall:i. Execute Board-approved strategy;ii. Coordinate all subsidiaries into a unified operating system;iii. Ensure delivery of mission-aligned outcomes across all sectors.

28.4.7 Control Over Subsidiaries

a) The Holding Company shall:i. Incorporate, own, and control all Operating Subsidiaries;ii. Define mandates, governance standards, and performance targets.

b) Board approval shall be required for all major subsidiary decisions, including:i. Expansion into new jurisdictions or sectors;ii. Financing exceeding five percent (5%) of assets;iii. Mergers, acquisitions, or divestments;iv. Licensing or transfer of intellectual property;v. Strategic long-term partnerships.

28.4.8 Strategic Integration Role

a) The Holding Company shall act as the central integrator of all systems, including but not limited to:i. Transport and mobility systems;ii. Energy infrastructure;iii. Digital and data systems;iv. Financial and payment systems;v. Industrial and production ecosystems.

b) It shall ensure all components operate as a unified, interoperable continental system.

28.4.9 Financial Discipline

a) The Holding Company shall operate under principles of:i. Commercial sustainability;ii. Long-term capital preservation;iii. Responsible reinvestment into mission-aligned projects.

b) Profits shall be allocated in accordance with:i. The economic rights defined herein;ii. Strategic directives of the Foundation;iii. Applicable governance controls.

28.4.10 Mission Protection and Limitations

a) The Holding Company shall not:i. Undertake activities inconsistent with the Foundation’s mission;ii. Engage in speculative or non-aligned ventures.

b) Any such action shall constitute a Mission-Threatening Action under Article LVIII and shall be subject to intervention, reversal, or sanction.

28.4.11 Accountability and Reporting

a) The Holding Company shall maintain:i. Full transparency to the Trust Structure and Steward President;ii. Regular financial, operational, and impact reporting.

b) It shall comply with:i. Independent audit requirements;ii. Public accountability mechanisms established by the Foundation.

28.5 THE JOWIH GROUP

28.5.1 Establishment

a) The “Jowih Group” is hereby established as the unified operational structure comprising all entities owned, controlled, or administered by Jowih Holdings Limited.

b) The Group shall serve as the primary execution framework through which the objectives of the Jowih Foundation are implemented across all sectors, jurisdictions, and development corridors.

c) The Jowih Group shall not constitute a separate legal entity but shall operate as an integrated organizational structure under the authority of Jowih Holdings Limited.

d) The Jowih Group shall be guided  under the private ANNEX J

28.5.2 Purpose

a) The purpose of the Jowih Group shall be to:i. Execute large-scale continental and global infrastructure systems;ii. Enable coordinated economic development across multiple sectors;iii. Integrate digital, financial, industrial, and physical systems into a unified ecosystem;iv. Facilitate partner-led execution in alignment with the principles of Development Neutrality;v. Operationalize the mission, frameworks, and directives of the Jowih Foundation.

28.5.3 Composition

a) The Jowih Group shall consist of:i. Core Operating Subsidiaries established under Jowih Holdings Limited;ii. Special Purpose Vehicles (SPVs) created for specific projects, corridors, or asset classes;iii. Joint Venture Entities formed with governments, institutions, or approved partners;iv. Regional and Country-Level Entities established to support localized execution.

b) The principal operating subsidiaries of the Group shall be defined in Schedule X of this Constitution.

28.5.4 Governance Authority

a) The Jowih Group shall not maintain an independent Board of Directors.

b) All governance authority over the Group shall reside exclusively with the Board of Jowih Holdings Limited.

c) The Board of Jowih Holdings Limited shall:i. Approve the establishment, restructuring, or dissolution of Group entities;ii. Exercise fiduciary oversight over all subsidiaries and associated structures;iii. Ensure alignment with the directives of the Jowih Foundation.

28.5.5 Group Leadership Structure

a) The operational leadership of the Jowih Group shall be vested in a Group Executive Structure appointed under Jowih Holdings Limited.

b) The Group Executive Structure shall include, at minimum:

i. Group Chief Executive Officer (GCEO);

ii. Group Chief Operating Officer (GCOO);

iii. Group Chief Financial Officer (GCFO);

iv. Chief Executives of the Core Operating Subsidiaries and such other principal operating entities as may be designated by the Board; and

v. such additional Group executive offices as may be established by the Board of Jowih Holdings Limited upon recommendation of the GCEO, where necessary for effective coordination, integration, research, innovation, technology, risk management, legal affairs, capital allocation, or other Group-wide functions.

c) Group executive offices established under paragraph (b)(v) shall not require amendment of this Constitution unless the office is expressly designated as a constitutional office or its creation materially alters the governance structure established herein.

28.5.6 Appointment and Accountability

a) The Group Chief Executive Officer shall be appointed by the Board of Jowih Holdings Limited, subject to alignment with the strategic direction of the Jowih Foundation.

b) All other Group Executives shall be appointed by the Board of Jowih Holdings Limited upon recommendation of the Group Chief Executive Officer.

c) Sector Chief Executives shall:i. Lead their respective subsidiaries;ii. Report operationally to the Group Chief Executive Officer;iii. Remain accountable to the Board through established governance structures.

d) All appointments shall be based on competence, alignment with mission objectives, and the capacity to execute at continental scale.

e) The establishment of a Group executive office shall not confer upon the office-holder any authority over the Foundation except as expressly delegated or provided by this Constitution.

28.5.7 Operational Authority

a) The Group Executive Structure shall be responsible for:i. Coordinating execution across all subsidiaries and sectors;ii. Implementing strategies approved by the Board;iii. Managing partnerships with governments, institutions, and private entities;iv. Ensuring efficient deployment of capital, infrastructure, and systems.

b) Operational authority shall not extend to altering the mission, ownership structures, or foundational principles established by the Jowih Foundation.

28.5.8 Strategic Alignment

a) All entities within the Jowih Group shall operate in strict alignment with:i. The mission and objectives of the Jowih Foundation;ii. The strategic direction issued by the Foundation;iii. The governance framework of Jowih Holdings Limited.

b) No Group entity shall undertake actions inconsistent with the principles of Development Neutrality, non-custodial financial operation, or partner-led execution.

28.5.9 Coordination and Integration

a) Jowih Holdings through Jowih Group  shall establish internal systems and mechanisms to ensure:i. Integration across all sectors and subsidiaries;ii. Interoperability of digital, financial, and physical infrastructure;iii. Standardization of protocols, systems, and operational frameworks;iv. Alignment with the Jowih digital ecosystem and other infrastructure.

28.5.10 Residual Authority

a) Any matter not expressly delegated to the Group Executive Structure shall remain under the authority of the Board of Jowih Holdings Limited.

b) The Board reserves the right to intervene, restructure, or assume direct control of any Group entity where necessary to protect the integrity, mission, or operational continuity of the Jowih system.

28.6 Dividend Flow Mechanism  

a) Operating Subsidiaries Distribute 100% of available dividends to Jowih Holdings Limited, after maintaining prudent reserves (minimum 20% of annual profits);

b) Jowih Holdings Limited Distributes dividends to shareholders: 90% to the Foundation and 10% to Founder Family Trust;

c) Annual Minimum: No less than 90% of Jowih Holdings Limited's consolidated net profits shall be distributed as dividends to Mission Fund in Foundation’s Treasury, subject to prudent reserve requirements and growth investment needs;

28.7 Legal Implementation 

a) All structures must be documented in binding instruments (trust deeds, shareholder registers, articles of association) that are legally enforceable in Mauritius, Singapore, and all operating jurisdictions;

b) All documents must cross-reference this Constitution and incorporate its protective provisions as fundamental clauses;

c) No changes to this ownership structure may be made without complying with Core Article amendment procedures (Article XIX);

d) The Foundation's Board shall maintain a Central Registry of all ownership documents, updated annually.

28.8 Perpetual Alignment 

This structure is established as a permanent feature and may not be altered in any way that:

a) Reduces the Foundation's economic interest below 90% of consolidated profits and Family trust 10%;

b) Diminishes the Purpose Trust's control over the Holding Company below 60% voting power and Family Trust 40%;

c) Bypasses the Holding Company's governance of subsidiaries;

d) Weakens the separation between mission control and economic benefits;

e) Compromises the mission-aligned dividend flow;

f) Reduces structural protections against mission drift.

28.9 Cross-Entity Enforcement

a) The Jowih Purpose Trust shall enforce mission alignment across Jowih Holdings Limited and all subsidiaries;

b) The Foundation's Board shall oversee compliance with this ownership structure through quarterly reviews;

c) The Independent Auditor General shall audit adherence to these provisions annually and report to the Oversight Panel;

d) Any breach shall trigger immediate remedial action under Article XLVIII procedures.

28.10 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein.

Article XXIX - Jowih Purpose Trust (Mission Guardian)

29.1 Establishment and Purpose

The Jowih Purpose Trust is an irrevocable trust established under Mauritius law to hold 60% of voting rights in Jowih Holdings Limited, thereby exercising mission control over the entire commercial ecosystem while remaining operationally detached. Its sole purpose is perpetual protection of the Foundation's Mission as defined in Article II.

29.2 Trustee Governance

a) Board Composition: 5 Independent Trustees, appointed as detailed in confidential ANNEX C.

b) Initial Appointment: First generation of Trustees appointed by the Founder  as detailed in ANNEX C.

c) Succession Mechanism: Thereafter new Trustees selected as detailed in confidential ANNEX C.

d) Ex Officio Role: The Steward President serves as ex officio observer with speaking rights but no voting authority.

e) Fiduciary Duty: Trustees owe exclusive duty to the Mission and this constitution, not to beneficiaries, with mission protection above all other considerations.

29.3 Trustee Oversight & Removal

(a) Trustees Oversight Committee (Enforcer):An independent body designated as the “Enforcer,” as required by law, shall oversee and monitor the conduct of Trustees. It shall comprise five (5) members: three (3) external governance experts appointed by the Combined Trusts Board and two (2) Foundation representatives. Detailed procedures shall be set out in confidential ANNEX C. The first generation of members may be appointed by the Founder. The Enforcer may engage independent legal counsel across relevant jurisdictions to support its functions.

(b) Removal for Cause Only:Trustees may be removed solely for documented cause, including political involvement,breach of duty, ethical violation, incapacity, or conflict of interest, and in accordance with applicable Mauritian law and court process where required. All removal actions shall be formally recorded and publicly disclosed. Any dispute between the Trusts regarding appointment or removal shall be resolved through binding mediation as provided in confidential ANNEX F.

(c) Appeal Process:A Trustee subject to removal shall have the right to appeal to the Supreme Court of Mauritius.

(d) Replacement:Vacancies arising from removal or resignation shall be filled in accordance with the procedures set out in confidential ANNEX C.

29.4 Powers and Authority  

The Purpose Trust shall and not limited to:  

a) Hold and exercise 60% voting rights in Jowih Holdings Limited through its appointed directors  

b) Appoint 4 directors to the Holding Company Board  

c) Vote on any Holding Company action in exception of potentially harming the Mission, with written justification required  

d) Ensure dividend flows comply with Article XXVIII.5 through quarterly reviews     

e) Require immediate corrective action for any mission deviation, including CEOs removal if necessary

f) Form a Combined Trust Board with Family Trust as mandated by this constitution

29.5 Limitations  

The Purpose Trust shall not:  

a) Engage in commercial activities or operational management of subsidiaries  

b) Directly appoint subsidiary CEOs or boards (delegated to Holding Company Board)  

c) Distribute profits or permit private gain from trust assets  

d) Amend its deed without tier 1 Core Article compliance (Article XIX)  

e) Interfere in day-to-day operations of Holding Company or subsidiaries  

f) Exercise powers for any purpose other than mission protection

29.6 Reporting and Transparency

a) Annual Report: Detailed mission compliance report published via Transparency Dashboard (Article XXXV) within 90 days of fiscal year-end  

b) Trustee Disclosures: All trustee selections, deliberations, and decisions affecting mission alignment must be publicly disclosed to avoid conflict of interest.

c) Financial Audit: Independent Auditor General verifies mission alignment and financial compliance annually  

d) Historical Archive: Maintains complete records of all trustee deliberations for future reference

29.7 Transition Protocol

a) Initial Trustees: First generation of 4 Trustees appointed by the Founder as detailed in ANNEX C.

b) Succession Activation: Following confidential ANNEX C, succession protocol activates and Trustees assume full self-perpetuation authority.

c) Foundational Documents: Original Mission statement and Trust documents preserved as reference for all future Trustees.

29.8 Core Article Status

This Article is designated as a Tier 1 Core Article under Article XIX.3 and may be amended only through the procedures therein.

Article XXX - Founder Family Trust (Legacy & Protection Layer)

30.1 Establishment and Purpose

The Founder Family Trust holds 10% economic rights and 40% strategic voting rights of Jowih Holdings Limited, providing:
